Source
Open problem. Whether an odd perfect number exists is unknown. This project does not claim a proof; it formalizes necessary conditions.
On this page
  • Foundations
  • Euler's form and the special prime
  • Prime factor counts, size and exponents
  • Exponent patterns
  • Collisions of x² + x + 1
  • Tools and countermodels

Foundations

Definitions, divisor sums of prime powers, valuations, and the divisor sum network.

OddPerfect.Basic

OddPerfect/Basic.lean · 43 lines · 6 public theorems · imports Seed.Starter

The two definitions everything is read against: OddPerfect N (N is odd and perfect, using mathlib's Nat.Perfect) and OddPerfectConjecture, a statement that is never assumed.

Proof idea. No arguments here: six one line projections such as σ 1 N = 2 * N unpack the definition.

OddPerfect.Seed.Starter

OddPerfect/Seed/Starter.lean · 116 lines · 7 public theorems

Exact prime power components of any perfect number: the forcing step and valuation conservation.

Proof idea. By multiplicativity, σ(pe) divides σ(n) = 2n when pe is the full p component of n. So every odd prime dividing σ(pe) divides n (force_prime), and comparing the q exponents of both sides of σ(n) = 2n gives the conservation identity valuation_balance. The examples: σ(3²) = 13 forces 13, σ(13²) = 3·61 forces 61, and the three squares 7², 13², 19² each put a factor 3 into σ(n), which is too many when 3 has exponent exactly 2.

Public theorems

OPNStarter.sigma_eq_two_mul

theorem sigma_eq_two_mul {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) : σ 1 n = 2 * n

OPNStarter.component_sigma_dvd

theorem component_sigma_dvd {n p e : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (hp : p.Prime)
  (he : n.factorization p = e) : σ 1 (p ^ e) ∣ 2 * n

The divisor sum of an entire prime-power component divides the full divisor sum.

OPNStarter.force_prime

theorem force_prime {n p e q : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (hp : p.Prime)
  (he : n.factorization p = e) (hq : q.Prime) (hodd : q ≠ 2)
  (hdiv : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) : q ∣ n

Any odd prime dividing the divisor sum of an exact component must divide the number.

OPNStarter.valuation_balance

theorem valuation_balance {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (q : ℕ) :
  Nat.factorization 2 q + n.factorization q =
    ∑ p ∈ n.primeFactors, (σ 1 (p ^ n.factorization p)).factorization q

A prime can occur several times among different component sums. This equality counts all incidences, including repeated factors, with the extra factor of two on the left.

OPNStarter.thirteen_dvd_of_three_sq

theorem thirteen_dvd_of_three_sq {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (he : n.factorization 3 = 2) :
  13 ∣ n

A certified first link of a factor chain.

OPNStarter.sixty_one_dvd_of_thirteen_sq

theorem sixty_one_dvd_of_thirteen_sq {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (he : n.factorization 13 = 2) :
  61 ∣ n

The second link requires its own exact-exponent hypothesis.

OPNStarter.exclude_four_exact_squares

theorem exclude_four_exact_squares {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect)
  (h3 : n.factorization 3 = 2) (h7 : n.factorization 7 = 2)
  (h13 : n.factorization 13 = 2) (h19 : n.factorization 19 = 2) : False

Three distinct exact squares each contribute a factor 3. Together they contradict an exponent of exactly two at 3, regardless of which other primes divide the number.

OddPerfect.Seed.Cut

OddPerfect/Seed/Cut.lean · 68 lines · 2 public theorems

For a perfect number split into two coprime factors a and b, some prime of a divides σ(b).

Proof idea. From σ(a)σ(b) = 2ab: if no prime of a divided σ(b), then a would divide σ(a), so σ(a) = ka with k ≥ 2 because σ(a) > a. Then σ(a)σ(b) ≥ 2aσ(b) > 2ab, a contradiction. Splitting σ(b) over the components of b gives one component of b whose divisor sum is divisible by a prime of a.

Public theorems

OPNCut.exists_crossing_prime

theorem exists_crossing_prime {a b : ℕ} (ha : 1 < a) (hb : 1 < b)
  (hc : a.Coprime b) (hperf : (a * b).Perfect) :
  ∃ r : ℕ, r.Prime ∧ r ∣ a ∧ r ∣ σ 1 b

One prime dividing the first factor also divides the divisor sum of the second. Swapping the factors gives the other direction of the cut.

OPNCut.exists_crossing_component

theorem exists_crossing_component {a b : ℕ} (ha : 1 < a) (hb : 1 < b)
  (hc : a.Coprime b) (hperf : (a * b).Perfect) :
  ∃ r ∈ a.primeFactors, ∃ p ∈ b.primeFactors,
    r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ b.factorization p)

A particular prime-power component on the second side supplies a crossing edge. Here the source exponent is its full exponent in the second factor.

OddPerfect.Seed.Example

OddPerfect/Seed/Example.lean · 90 lines · 3 public theorems · imports Seed.Starter

No perfect number has the exponents of both 3 and 5 congruent to 2 modulo 3; also the divisibility of geometric sums used everywhere.

Proof idea. A geometric sum of length d divides one of length any multiple of d, so σ(pa) divides σ(pe) when a + 1 divides e + 1. With exponents ≡ 2 (mod 3), σ(3²) = 13 and σ(5²) = 31 divide the component sums, so 13 and 31 divide n. If 13 has exponent 1, σ(13) = 14 forces 7 and 3²·5²·13·7 is an abundant divisor; otherwise 3²·5²·13²·31 is abundant.

Public theorems

OPNExample.geom_sum_dvd_geom_sum

theorem geom_sum_dvd_geom_sum {d m : ℕ} (p : ℕ) (hd : d ∣ m) :
  (∑ i ∈ Finset.range d, p ^ i) ∣ ∑ i ∈ Finset.range m, p ^ i

A geometric sum of length d divides every sum whose length is a multiple of d.

OPNExample.sigma_prime_pow_dvd

theorem sigma_prime_pow_dvd {p a e : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (h : a + 1 ∣ e + 1) :
  σ 1 (p ^ a) ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)

If a+1 divides the true component length e+1, the divisor sum of p^a divides that of p^e.

OPNExample.exclude_three_and_five_exponents

theorem exclude_three_and_five_exponents {n : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect)
  (h3 : n.factorization 3 % 3 = 2) (h5 : n.factorization 5 % 3 = 2) : False

No perfect number has the exponents of both 3 and 5 congruent to 2 modulo 3.

OddPerfect.Abundancy

OddPerfect/Abundancy.lean · 236 lines · 15 public theorems · imports Basic

The abundancy σ(n)/n as an exact rational: multiplicativity, monotonicity, the bound p/(p − 1), and at least three prime factors.

Proof idea. Abundancy is a product over prime power components, each of which is a geometric sum in 1/p and lies strictly below p/(p − 1). A divisor with abundancy above 2 excludes perfection. For three prime factors: odd primes give p/(p − 1) ≤ 3/2 for p = 3 and ≤ 5/4 for every other prime, so with at most two prime factors the product is at most 15/8 < 2.

Public theorems

abundancy_eq_sum_inv

theorem abundancy_eq_sum_inv {n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) :
  abundancy n = ∑ e ∈ n.divisors, (e : ℚ)⁻¹

For n ≠ 0, the abundancy is the sum of the reciprocals of the divisors.

abundancy_mul_of_coprime

theorem abundancy_mul_of_coprime {a b : ℕ} (h : a.Coprime b) :
  abundancy (a * b) = abundancy a * abundancy b

Abundancy is multiplicative on coprime arguments.

abundancy_prime_pow

theorem abundancy_prime_pow {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  abundancy (p ^ a) = ∑ i ∈ Finset.range (a + 1), ((p : ℚ)⁻¹) ^ i

Abundancy of a prime power as a geometric sum in 1/p.

abundancy_prime_pow_lt

theorem abundancy_prime_pow_lt {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  abundancy (p ^ a) < (p : ℚ) / (p - 1)

Abundancy of a prime power is strictly below p/(p-1).

abundancy_prime_pow_mono

theorem abundancy_prime_pow_mono {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) {a b : ℕ} (h : a ≤ b) :
  abundancy (p ^ a) ≤ abundancy (p ^ b)

Abundancy of p ^ a is monotone in a.

one_add_inv_le_abundancy_prime_pow

theorem one_add_inv_le_abundancy_prime_pow {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) {a : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) :
  1 + (p : ℚ)⁻¹ ≤ abundancy (p ^ a)

A positive power of p has abundancy at least 1 + 1/p.

abundancy_le_of_dvd

theorem abundancy_le_of_dvd {d n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) (hd : d ∣ n) :
  abundancy d ≤ abundancy n

Abundancy is monotone along divisibility.

abundancy_lt_of_dvd_of_ne

theorem abundancy_lt_of_dvd_of_ne {d n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) (hd : d ∣ n) (hne : d ≠ n) :
  abundancy d < abundancy n

Abundancy is strictly monotone along proper divisibility.

perfect_iff_abundancy_eq_two

theorem perfect_iff_abundancy_eq_two {n : ℕ} (hn : 0 < n) :
  n.Perfect ↔ abundancy n = 2

A positive integer is perfect exactly when its abundancy is two.

abundancy_eq_prod_primeFactors

theorem abundancy_eq_prod_primeFactors {n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) :
  abundancy n = ∏ p ∈ n.primeFactors, abundancy (p ^ n.factorization p)

Abundancy is the product of the abundancies of the prime power components.

abundancy_lt_prod

theorem abundancy_lt_prod {n : ℕ} (hn : 1 < n) :
  abundancy n < ∏ p ∈ n.primeFactors, ((p : ℚ) / (p - 1))

For n > 1, abundancy is strictly below ∏ p/(p-1) over its prime factors.

not_perfect_of_dvd_of_two_lt_abundancy

theorem not_perfect_of_dvd_of_two_lt_abundancy {d n : ℕ} (hd : d ∣ n)
  (h : 2 < abundancy d) : ¬ n.Perfect

A divisor with abundancy above two excludes perfection.

not_perfect_of_dvd_of_two_le_abundancy

theorem not_perfect_of_dvd_of_two_le_abundancy {d n : ℕ} (hd : d ∣ n) (hne : d ≠ n)
  (h : 2 ≤ abundancy d) : ¬ n.Perfect

A proper divisor with abundancy at least two excludes perfection.

not_perfect_of_prod_le_two

theorem not_perfect_of_prod_le_two {n : ℕ} (hn : 1 < n)
  (h : ∏ p ∈ n.primeFactors, ((p : ℚ) / (p - 1)) ≤ 2) : ¬ n.Perfect

If the full prime support cannot reach abundancy two, the number is not perfect.

OddPerfect.three_le_card_primeFactors

theorem three_le_card_primeFactors {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) : 3 ≤ N.primeFactors.card

An odd perfect number has at least three distinct prime factors.

OddPerfect.Cyclotomic

OddPerfect/Cyclotomic.lean · 179 lines · 7 public theorems · imports Basic

σ(pa) as a product of cyclotomic values, its closed form, and the arithmetic of its prime divisors.

Proof idea. σ(pa)·(p − 1) = pa+1 − 1 and the cyclotomic factorization over the divisors of a + 1 other than 1. A prime q dividing σ(pa) differs from p, and either p ≡ 1 (mod q) with q dividing a + 1, or the order of p modulo q exceeds 1 and divides both a + 1 and q − 1. For an odd prime q dividing p − 1, lifting the exponent gives vq(σ(pa)) = vq(a + 1).

Public theorems

OddPerfect.sigma_prime_pow_eq_prod_cyclotomic

theorem sigma_prime_pow_eq_prod_cyclotomic {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  ((σ 1 (p ^ a) : ℕ) : ℤ) =
    ∏ d ∈ (a + 1).divisors.erase 1, (Polynomial.cyclotomic d ℤ).eval (p : ℤ)

The divisor sum of a prime power factors as a product of cyclotomic values.

OddPerfect.sigma_prime_pow_mul_pred

theorem sigma_prime_pow_mul_pred {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  σ 1 (p ^ a) * (p - 1) = p ^ (a + 1) - 1

Closed form of the divisor sum of a prime power.

OddPerfect.prime_dvd_sigma_prime_pow

theorem prime_dvd_sigma_prime_pow {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hdiv : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ a)) :
  q ≠ p ∧
  ((p % q = 1 ∧ q ∣ a + 1) ∨
   (1 < orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∧ orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ a + 1 ∧
     orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ q - 1))

A prime divisor q of σ 1 (p ^ a) differs from p, and either p ≡ 1 (mod q) with q ∣ a + 1, or the order of p modulo q exceeds 1 and divides both a + 1 and q - 1.

OddPerfect.prime_dvd_sigma_prime_pow_of_prime_length

theorem prime_dvd_sigma_prime_pow_of_prime_length {p q l : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hl : l.Prime) (hdiv : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ (l - 1))) : q = l ∨ q % l = 1

Every prime divisor of σ 1 (p ^ (l - 1)), for a prime length l, is l itself or congruent to 1 modulo l.

OddPerfect.dvd_sigma_prime_pow_of_orderOf_dvd

theorem dvd_sigma_prime_pow_of_orderOf_dvd {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hpq : p ≠ q) (h1 : 1 < orderOf (p : ZMod q)) (hd : orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ a + 1) :
  q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ a)

If the order of p modulo a prime q exceeds 1 and divides a + 1, then q divides σ 1 (p ^ a).

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_dvd_pred

theorem padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_dvd_pred {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (h : q ∣ p - 1) :
  padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ a)) = padicValNat q (a + 1)

Lifting the exponent: for an odd prime q dividing p - 1, the q-adic valuation of σ 1 (p ^ a) equals that of a + 1.

OddPerfect.orderOf_eq_of_dvd_cyclotomic_eval

theorem orderOf_eq_of_dvd_cyclotomic_eval {p q d : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime)
  (hdiv : (q : ℤ) ∣ (Polynomial.cyclotomic d ℤ).eval (p : ℤ)) (hqd : ¬ q ∣ d) :
  orderOf (p : ZMod q) = d ∧ d ∣ q - 1

If a prime q not dividing d divides the dth cyclotomic value at p, then the order of p modulo q is exactly d, and hence d ∣ q - 1.

OddPerfect.Valuation

OddPerfect/Valuation.lean · 282 lines · 15 public theorems · imports Cyclotomic, Network

Exact q-adic valuations of σ(pa) for every prime q and every exponent, and the 2-adic and 3-adic ledgers they give.

Proof idea. Through the order d of p modulo q: if d does not divide a + 1 the valuation is 0; otherwise it is vq(pd − 1) + vq((a + 1)/d). Closed forms at q = 2 (zero for even a, v₂(p + 1) + v₂(a + 1) − 1 for odd a) and at q = 3. Applied to the network, the 2-adic ledger shows the special component alone carries the single factor 2, with v₂(s + 1) = v₂(k + 1) = 1.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_self

theorem padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_self {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  padicValNat p (σ 1 (p ^ a)) = 0

q = p: the prime p never divides σ 1 (p ^ a).

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_orderOf_dvd

theorem padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_orderOf_dvd {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (hpq : p ≠ q) (h1 : 1 < orderOf (p : ZMod q))
  (hd : orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ a + 1) :
  padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ a)) =
    padicValNat q (p ^ orderOf (p : ZMod q) - 1) +
      padicValNat q ((a + 1) / orderOf (p : ZMod q))

Odd q, general order: if the order d of p modulo q exceeds 1 and divides a + 1, then v_q(σ(p ^ a)) = v_q(p ^ d - 1) + v_q((a + 1) / d).

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_not_orderOf_dvd

theorem padicValNat_sigma_prime_pow_of_not_orderOf_dvd {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime)
  (hq : q.Prime) (hd : ¬ orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ a + 1) :
  padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ a)) = 0

If the order of p modulo a prime q does not divide a + 1, then q does not divide σ 1 (p ^ a).

OddPerfect.dvd_sigma_prime_pow_iff

theorem dvd_sigma_prime_pow_iff {p q a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hpq : p ≠ q) :
  q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ a) ↔
    (if orderOf (p : ZMod q) = 1 then q ∣ a + 1 else orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ a + 1)

Unified divisibility criterion: with d the order of p modulo a prime q ≠ p, q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ a) iff q ∣ a + 1 when d = 1, and iff d ∣ a + 1 otherwise.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_two_sigma_prime_pow

theorem padicValNat_two_sigma_prime_pow {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hp2 : p ≠ 2) (a : ℕ) :
  padicValNat 2 (σ 1 (p ^ a)) =
    if Even a then 0 else padicValNat 2 (p + 1) + padicValNat 2 (a + 1) - 1

q = 2: for an odd prime p, σ 1 (p ^ a) is odd for even a, and for odd a its 2-adic valuation is v_2(p + 1) + v_2(a + 1) - 1.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow_of_mod_one

theorem padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow_of_mod_one {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (h : p % 3 = 1)
  (a : ℕ) : padicValNat 3 (σ 1 (p ^ a)) = padicValNat 3 (a + 1)

q = 3, p ≡ 1 (mod 3): v_3(σ(p ^ a)) = v_3(a + 1).

OddPerfect.padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow_of_mod_two

theorem padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow_of_mod_two {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (h : p % 3 = 2)
  (a : ℕ) : padicValNat 3 (σ 1 (p ^ a)) =
    if Even a then 0 else padicValNat 3 (p + 1) + padicValNat 3 (a + 1)

q = 3, p ≡ 2 (mod 3): v_3(σ(p ^ a)) is 0 for even a and v_3(p + 1) + v_3(a + 1) for odd a.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_three_sigma_three_pow

theorem padicValNat_three_sigma_three_pow (a : ℕ) : padicValNat 3 (σ 1 (3 ^ a)) = 0

q = 3, p = 3: 3 never divides σ 1 (3 ^ a).

OddPerfect.padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow

theorem padicValNat_three_sigma_prime_pow {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (a : ℕ) :
  padicValNat 3 (σ 1 (p ^ a)) = threeWeight p a

v_3(σ(p ^ a)) = threeWeight p a for every prime p and every exponent a.

OddPerfect.sigmaWeight_eq_padicValNat

theorem sigmaWeight_eq_padicValNat (N p : ℕ) {q : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime) :
  sigmaWeight N p q = padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p))

The network weight is the q-adic valuation of the component divisor sum.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_two_special

theorem padicValNat_two_special {N s : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hs : s.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization s)) :
  padicValNat 2 (s + 1) = 1 ∧ padicValNat 2 (N.factorization s + 1) = 1

The special prime s of an odd perfect number, with odd exponent k, satisfies v_2(s + 1) = 1 and v_2(k + 1) = 1: the special component alone supplies the single unit of the 2-adic ledger.

OddPerfect.mod_four_special

theorem mod_four_special {N s : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hs : s.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization s)) : s % 4 = 1 ∧ N.factorization s % 4 = 1

Consistency with Euler: the special prime and its exponent are both ≡ 1 (mod 4), derived from the 2-adic ledger.

OddPerfect.factorization_three_eq_sum_threeWeight

theorem factorization_three_eq_sum_threeWeight {N : ℕ} (hN : N.Perfect) :
  N.factorization 3 = ∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, threeWeight p (N.factorization p)

The 3-adic ledger of a perfect number in closed form.

OddPerfect.factorization_eq_sum_erase

theorem factorization_eq_sum_erase {N q : ℕ} (hN : N.Perfect) (hq : q.Prime) (hq2 : q ≠ 2) :
  N.factorization q =
    ∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors.erase q, padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p))

The q-adic ledger of a perfect number at an odd prime q, with the vanishing self term removed.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_pow_orderOf_sub_one_le

theorem padicValNat_pow_orderOf_sub_one_le {N p q : ℕ} (hN : N.Perfect) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hpq : p ≠ q) (h1 : 1 < orderOf (p : ZMod q))
  (hd : orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ N.factorization p + 1) :
  padicValNat q (p ^ orderOf (p : ZMod q) - 1) ≤ N.factorization q

Lower bound: if a prime factor p ≠ q has order d > 1 modulo an odd prime q and d ∣ v_p(N) + 1, then v_q(p ^ d - 1) ≤ v_q(N).

OddPerfect.Phi3

OddPerfect/Phi3.lean · 226 lines · 23 public theorems · imports Network, Seed.ThreeFree, Seed.Collision

Arithmetic of σ(p²) = p² + p + 1: parity, coprimality, the exact power of 3, residues of other prime divisors, and roots modulo a prime.

Proof idea. p² + p + 1 is odd, coprime to p and p + 1, divisible by 3 exactly when p ≡ 1 (mod 3) and never by 9. Any other prime divisor has p of order 3, hence is ≡ 1 (mod 6). It lies strictly between p² and (p + 1)², so it is not a square. Modulo a prime there are at most two roots, and two roots below the prime sum to the prime minus 1. Consequence: each exact square prime ≡ 1 (mod 3) sends exactly one factor 3, so their number is at most v₃(N).

OddPerfect.Network

OddPerfect/Network.lean · 224 lines · 9 public theorems · imports Basic, Seed.Cut

The divisor sum network of a perfect number: weights, conservation, no self loops, and strong connectivity.

Proof idea. Conservation at an odd prime q is valuation_balance with vq(2) = 0, and at 2 the incoming weights sum to 1 because N is odd. A prime never divides σ(pe), so there are no self loops. For connectivity, any nonempty proper set S of prime factors splits N into the coprime parts over S and over its complement, and the cut lemma gives an edge from the complement into S; so the set of primes that reach a given prime cannot be proper.

Conservation at an odd prime: the incoming weights add up to the exponent of q.

For any nonempty proper subset S of the prime factors there is an edge from the complement into S. Applying this to the complement gives an edge out of S.

OddPerfect.Reduction

OddPerfect/Reduction.lean · 263 lines · 10 public theorems · imports Euler, Abundancy, Network

Balanced candidates are exactly the factorizations of odd perfect numbers, so the conjecture is equivalent to their nonexistence.

Proof idea. One direction: an odd perfect number's own factorization is balanced by the conservation laws of Network. The other: for a balanced candidate, compare the q exponent of σ(value) with that of 2·value for every q. Multiplicativity turns the left side into the sum of the weights into q, and balance makes this equal to the exponent of q (or 1 at q = 2, or 0 at non primes). Equal factorizations give σ(value) = 2·value. This is a reformulation, not progress toward a proof.

Euler's form and the special prime

Consequences of the shape N = qkm².

OddPerfect.Euler

OddPerfect/Euler.lean · 199 lines · 7 public theorems · imports Basic

Euler's form N = qkm² with q ≡ k ≡ 1 (mod 4), N ≡ 1 (mod 4), N not a square, and Touchard's congruence.

Proof idea. The 2-adic ledger: the weights into 2 sum to 1, and σ(pe) is even exactly when e is odd, so exactly one prime q has odd exponent k and v₂(σ(qk)) = 1. If q ≡ 3 (mod 4), pairing terms shows 4 divides σ(qk); with q ≡ 1 (mod 4), σ(qk) ≡ k + 1 (mod 4) forces k ≡ 1 (mod 4). Touchard: if 3 divides N then 9 does (3 ≡ 3 mod 4 is not special), giving N ≡ 9 (mod 36); if N ≡ 2 (mod 3) then q ≡ 2 (mod 3), and 3 divides q + 1, which divides σ(qk), so 3 would divide N.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.exists_special_prime

theorem exists_special_prime (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ q : ℕ, q.Prime ∧ q ∣ N ∧ q % 4 = 1 ∧ N.factorization q % 4 = 1 ∧
    ∀ p : ℕ, p.Prime → p ≠ q → Even (N.factorization p)

Euler: an odd perfect number has a unique prime q of odd exponent, and q ≡ 1 (mod 4) and its exponent is ≡ 1 (mod 4).

OddPerfect.existsUnique_odd_exponent

theorem existsUnique_odd_exponent (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃! q : ℕ, q.Prime ∧ Odd (N.factorization q)

An odd perfect number has exactly one prime with odd exponent.

OddPerfect.exists_euler_form

theorem exists_euler_form (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ q k m : ℕ, q.Prime ∧ N = q ^ k * m ^ 2 ∧ ¬ q ∣ m ∧ q % 4 = 1 ∧ k % 4 = 1

Euler form: N = q ^ k * m ^ 2 with q prime, q ∤ m, and q ≡ k ≡ 1 (mod 4).

Proof idea. The 2-adic ledger. N is odd, so σ(N) = 2N has exactly one factor 2, shared among the components σ(pe). For odd p, σ(pe) has e + 1 odd terms, so it is even exactly when e is odd: one prime q has odd exponent k, and v₂(σ(qk)) = 1. If q ≡ 3 (mod 4), pairing consecutive terms puts q + 1, a multiple of 4, into σ(qk). So q ≡ 1 (mod 4), then σ(qk) ≡ k + 1 (mod 4) forces k ≡ 1 (mod 4).

An odd perfect number is ≡ 1 (mod 4).

An odd perfect number is not a perfect square.

  (hp : p.Prime) (hpN : p ∣ N) (h3 : p % 4 = 3) : p ^ 2 ∣ N

A prime p ≡ 3 (mod 4) dividing an odd perfect number divides it to at least the second power.

OddPerfect.SpecialPrime

OddPerfect/SpecialPrime.lean · 289 lines · 17 public theorems · imports Euler, Abundancy, Seed.Example

Facts about the special prime: q + 1 divides σ(qk), (q + 1)/2 divides N, abundancy splits, refined Touchard congruence, and small abundant divisors such as 3²·5·7².

Proof idea. Since k is odd, q + 1 divides σ(qk), which divides 2N. The special component has abundancy below q/(q − 1) ≤ 5/4, so the square part m² has abundancy above 2/(5/4) = 8/5. The refinement of Touchard: if v₃(N) = 2 then σ(3²) = 13 divides N, giving N ≡ 117 (mod 468); otherwise 3⁴ divides N, giving N ≡ 81 (mod 324). For 105: the primes 3 and 7 are ≡ 3 (mod 4), hence squared, and 3²·5·7² has σ = 4446 > 4410 = 2·2205.

OddPerfect.mod_refined

theorem mod_refined (h : OddPerfect N) :
  N % 12 = 1 ∨ N % 468 = 117 ∨ N % 324 = 81

Refinement of Touchard's congruence.

Proof idea. From N ≡ 1 (mod 4) and Touchard's N ≡ 1 (mod 12) or N ≡ 9 (mod 36). If 3 divides N, its exponent is even. If it is exactly 2, then σ(3²) = 13 divides N, and N is a multiple of 9·13 = 117 that is ≡ 1 (mod 4), so N ≡ 117 (mod 468). Otherwise 3⁴ divides N and N ≡ 81 (mod 324).

OddPerfect.not_105_dvd

theorem not_105_dvd (h : OddPerfect N) : ¬ 105 ∣ N

The classical obstruction: an odd perfect number is not divisible by 105.

Proof idea. The primes 3 and 7 are ≡ 3 (mod 4), so they cannot be special and appear at least squared. Then 3²·5·7² divides N, and σ(3²·5·7²) = 13·6·57 = 4446 exceeds 2·2205 = 4410: an abundant divisor, impossible for a perfect number.

OddPerfect.not_seven_dvd_of_three_five

theorem not_seven_dvd_of_three_five (h : OddPerfect N) (h3 : 3 ∣ N) (h5 : 5 ∣ N) :
  ¬ 7 ∣ N

Restatement of not_105_dvd.

  ¬ (3 ∣ N ∧ 5 ∣ N ∧ 11 ∣ N ∧ 13 ∣ N)

An odd perfect number is not divisible by all of 3, 5, 11, and 13.

OddPerfect.EulerPart

OddPerfect/EulerPart.lean · 460 lines · 22 public theorems · imports SpecialPrime, ClosedLoop, Valuation

Identities and congruences for Euler's form: the index σ(m²)/qk, the deficiency 2m² − σ(m²), congruences modulo 8, and small bounds.

Proof idea. Multiplicativity turns σ(N) = 2N into σ(qk)σ(m²) = 2qkm². Since qk is coprime to σ(qk), it divides σ(m²), which defines the index i with σ(m²) = qki and σ(qk)i = 2m². The index is odd because σ(m²) is odd: every exponent in m² is even, so each component sum has an odd number of odd terms. The deficiency identity is algebra: 2m² − σ(m²) = i(σ(qk) − qk) = iσ(qk−1). The congruence N ≡ q (mod 8) uses that m² ≡ 1 and, k being odd, qk ≡ q modulo 8.

OddPerfect.sigma_sq_mod_four_eq_one_iff

theorem sigma_sq_mod_four_eq_one_iff (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hN : N = q ^ k * m ^ 2) (hqm : ¬ q ∣ m) : σ 1 (m ^ 2) % 4 = 1 ↔ q % 8 = k % 8

σ(m ^ 2) ≡ 1 (mod 4) iff q ≡ k (mod 8).

OddPerfect.special_pow_add_eight_le

  (hN : N = q ^ k * m ^ 2) (hqm : ¬ q ∣ m) : q ^ k + 8 ≤ m ^ 2

m ^ 2 - q ^ k ≥ 8, that is, q ^ k + 8 ≤ m ^ 2.

OddPerfect.exponent_eq_one_of_lt

If m < q then k = 1.

OddPerfect.not_two_primes_exact_once

  (hpr : p ≠ r) (hp1 : N.factorization p = 1) (hr1 : N.factorization r = 1) : False

No two distinct primes divide an odd perfect number exactly once.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_pow_pred_le

OddPerfect.not_sq_dvd_sigma_pow_pred

For an odd prime l and any prime p, l ^ 2 ∤ σ(p ^ (l - 1)).

OddPerfect.not_twentyfive_dvd_sigma_pow_four

25 ∤ σ(p ^ 4) for every prime p.

OddPerfect.pow_pred_dvd_succ_of_sigma_eq_pow

theorem pow_pred_dvd_succ_of_sigma_eq_pow {p q l b a : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hp2 : p ≠ 2)
  (hq : q.Prime) (hσ : σ 1 (p ^ l) = q ^ b) (hd : p ^ l ∣ σ 1 (q ^ a)) :
  p ^ (l - 1) ∣ a + 1

For an odd prime p and a prime q, if σ(p ^ λ) = q ^ β and p ^ λ ∣ σ(q ^ α), then p ^ (λ - 1) ∣ α + 1. (False for p = 2: σ(2 ^ 4) = 31 and 2 ^ 4 ∣ σ(31 ^ 1) = 32, but 2 ^ 3 ∤ 2.)

OddPerfect.ClosedLoop

OddPerfect/ClosedLoop.lean · 321 lines · 5 public theorems · imports Euler, Network, Cyclotomic

The divisor sums of the ordinary components cannot all be powers of the special prime; consequences σ(m²) ≠ qk and 3qk < 2m².

Proof idea. Suppose σ(rer) = qcr for every ordinary r. Then the ordinary primes receive weight only from q, so rer divides σ(qk), which divides qk+1 − 1. Since qcr = 1 + r·σ(rer−1) is 1 modulo r exactly once, lifting the exponent gives rer−1 dividing k + 1, and with k odd, 2∏ rer−1 divides k + 1. Conservation at q gives k = Σ cr, while a size comparison gives cr < rer−1. With tr = rer−1 this gives k + 1 ≤ 1 + Σ (tr − 1) ≤ ∏ tr ≤ (k + 1)/2, a contradiction.

OddPerfect.not_all_components_special_powers

theorem not_all_components_special_powers {N q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q))
  (hpow : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → ∃ c : ℕ, σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p) = q ^ c) :
  False

In an odd perfect number, the divisor sums of the components other than the one with odd exponent cannot all be powers of that special prime.

OddPerfect.not_sigma_sq_eq_special_pow

theorem not_sigma_sq_eq_special_pow {N q k m : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)

  σ 1 (m ^ 2) ≠ q ^ k

In the Euler form N = q ^ k * m ^ 2 of an odd perfect number, σ(m ^ 2) is not equal to q ^ k.

OddPerfect.three_mul_sigma_special_le

theorem three_mul_sigma_special_le {N q k m : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hN : N = q ^ k * m ^ 2) (hqm : ¬ q ∣ m) (hk : Odd k) :
  3 * σ 1 (q ^ k) ≤ 2 * m ^ 2

3 σ(q ^ k) ≤ 2 m ^ 2 in the Euler form of an odd perfect number.

OddPerfect.special_pow_lt_sq

theorem special_pow_lt_sq {N q k m : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hN : N = q ^ k * m ^ 2) (hqm : ¬ q ∣ m) (hk : Odd k) : 3 * q ^ k < 2 * m ^ 2

In the Euler form of an odd perfect number, 3 q ^ k < 2 m ^ 2.

Proof idea. From σ(qk)σ(m²) = 2qkm² and q not dividing σ(qk), write σ(m²) = qkt, so σ(qk)t = 2m². Here t is odd (σ(qk) is even and m is odd), and t ≠ 1 because σ(m²) = qk is excluded by the closed loop argument. So t ≥ 3 and 3qk < 3σ(qk) ≤ 2m².

OddPerfect.exists_euler_form_with_bound

theorem exists_euler_form_with_bound {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ q k m : ℕ, q.Prime ∧ N = q ^ k * m ^ 2 ∧ ¬ q ∣ m ∧ q % 4 = 1 ∧ k % 4 = 1 ∧
    3 * q ^ k < 2 * m ^ 2

Every odd perfect number has an Euler form q ^ k * m ^ 2 with q ≡ k ≡ 1 (mod 4) and 3 q ^ k < 2 m ^ 2.

OddPerfect.Reciprocity

OddPerfect/Reciprocity.lean · 277 lines · 15 public theorems · imports Network, Valuation, Cyclotomic, SpecialPrime contains novelty unverified

Quadratic residue constraints on the network, after Wallace (arXiv 1610.00544): one sign identity per vertex, a reverse edge constraint, and two mutually residual prime factors.

Proof idea. σ(pa) ≡ 1 (mod p), so its Legendre symbol modulo p is 1; expanding over its factorization gives ∏ (r/p)w(p,r) = 1 at an ordinary vertex and (2/q) at the special vertex. In the other direction, if e is even and r divides σ(pe), then pe+1 ≡ 1 (mod r) with e + 1 odd, so p ≡ (pe/2+1)² is a square modulo r. Applied to an edge into the special prime q ≡ 1 (mod 4), reciprocity makes q a square modulo p as well.

The divisor sum of a prime power is a quadratic residue modulo the prime.

OddPerfect.legendreSym_eq_prod_factorization

  legendreSym p n = ∏ r ∈ n.primeFactors, legendreSym p r ^ n.factorization r

The Legendre symbol of a positive integer expands over its factorization.

OddPerfect.legendreSym_eq_one_of_dvd_sigma_even

theorem legendreSym_eq_one_of_dvd_sigma_even {p r e : ℕ} [Fact r.Prime] (hp : p.Prime)
  (he : Even e) (hr : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) : legendreSym r p = 1

Reverse edge constraint. If e is even and the prime r divides σ(p ^ e), then p is a quadratic residue modulo r: p ^ (e + 1) ≡ 1 (mod r) and e + 1 is odd.

OddPerfect.legendreSym_receiver_of_dvd_sigma_even

theorem legendreSym_receiver_of_dvd_sigma_even {p r e : ℕ} [Fact p.Prime] [Fact r.Prime]
  (hp2 : p ≠ 2) (hr2 : r ≠ 2) (he : Even e) (hr : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) :
  legendreSym p r = if p % 4 = 3 ∧ r % 4 = 3 then -1 else 1

With reciprocity, the reverse edge constraint fixes the symbol on the edge: if e is even and the odd prime r divides σ(p ^ e) for an odd prime p, then (r / p) = -1 exactly when p ≡ r ≡ 3 (mod 4), and (r / p) = 1 otherwise.

OddPerfect.legendreSym_two_pow_mul_prod_sigmaWeight

theorem legendreSym_two_pow_mul_prod_sigmaWeight {N p : ℕ} [Fact p.Prime] (h : OddPerfect N)
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) :
  legendreSym p 2 ^ sigmaWeight N p 2 *
    ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, legendreSym p r ^ sigmaWeight N p r = 1

The basic vertex identity in an odd perfect number: for every prime factor p, (2 / p) ^ w(p, 2) * ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, (r / p) ^ w(p, r) = 1, where w(p, r) = sigmaWeight N p r is the exponent of r in σ(p ^ v_p(N)).

OddPerfect.prod_legendreSym_sigmaWeight_of_even

theorem prod_legendreSym_sigmaWeight_of_even {N p : ℕ} [Fact p.Prime] (h : OddPerfect N)
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (he : Even (N.factorization p)) :
  ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, legendreSym p r ^ sigmaWeight N p r = 1

Non-special vertex: if v_p(N) is even then ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, (r / p) ^ w(p, r) = 1.

OddPerfect.sigmaWeight_special_two

theorem sigmaWeight_special_two {N q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hqN : q ∈ N.primeFactors) (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) : sigmaWeight N q 2 = 1

The special component carries exactly one factor 2.

OddPerfect.prod_legendreSym_sigmaWeight_special

theorem prod_legendreSym_sigmaWeight_special {N q : ℕ} [Fact q.Prime] (h : OddPerfect N)
  (hqN : q ∈ N.primeFactors) (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) :
  ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, legendreSym q r ^ sigmaWeight N q r =
    if q % 8 = 1 then 1 else -1

Special vertex: if q is the special prime, then ∏ r ∈ N.primeFactors, (r / q) ^ w(q, r) is 1 when q ≡ 1 (mod 8) and -1 otherwise (the only other case being q ≡ 5 (mod 8)).

OddPerfect.prod_pow_eq_neg_one_pow_card

theorem prod_pow_eq_neg_one_pow_card (S : Finset ℕ) (f : ℕ → ℤ) (w : ℕ → ℕ)
  (hf : ∀ r ∈ S, w r ≠ 0 → f r = 1 ∨ f r = -1) :
  ∏ r ∈ S, f r ^ w r = (-1) ^ (S.filter fun r => Odd (w r) ∧ f r = -1).card

A product of signs counts the odd exponent sign changes.

OddPerfect.odd_card_nonresidue_of_special_mod_eight_eq_five

theorem odd_card_nonresidue_of_special_mod_eight_eq_five {N q : ℕ} [Fact q.Prime]
  (h : OddPerfect N) (hqN : q ∈ N.primeFactors) (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q))
  (h8 : q % 8 = 5) :
  Odd (N.primeFactors.filter fun r => Odd (sigmaWeight N q r) ∧ legendreSym q r = -1).card

If the special prime q is ≡ 5 (mod 8), then an odd number of the prime factors r of N that occur to an odd power in σ(q ^ v_q(N)) are quadratic non-residues modulo q.

OddPerfect.exists_mutual_quadratic_residuesnovelty unverified

theorem exists_mutual_quadratic_residues {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ p ∈ N.primeFactors, ∃ r ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ r ∧
    jacobiSym p r = 1 ∧ jacobiSym r p = 1

Every odd perfect number has two distinct prime factors p and r with p a nonzero square modulo r and r a nonzero square modulo p. One can take r to be the special prime and p any prime factor whose component divisor sum is divisible by r.

Proof idea. The special prime q has positive exponent, so by conservation some prime p ≠ q sends weight to q. Its exponent e is even, so pe+1 ≡ 1 (mod q) with e + 1 odd, which makes p a square modulo q. Since q ≡ 1 (mod 4), quadratic reciprocity makes q a square modulo p.

OddPerfect.not_forall_jacobiSym_eq_neg_one

theorem not_forall_jacobiSym_eq_neg_one {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ¬ ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ r → jacobiSym p r = -1

It is impossible that every prime factor of an odd perfect number is a quadratic non-residue modulo every other one.

OddPerfect.prime_dvd_of_dvd_sigma_special

theorem prime_dvd_of_dvd_sigma_special {N q k m p : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hN : N = q ^ k * m ^ 2) (hqm : ¬ q ∣ m) (hp : p.Prime) (hp2 : p ≠ 2)
  (hd : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ k)) : p ∣ m

In Euler's form N = q ^ k * m ^ 2, every odd prime dividing σ(q ^ k) divides m.

Prime factor counts, size and exponents

Lower bounds on ω, inequalities between ω and Ω, upper bounds on N, and caps on exponents.

OddPerfect.OmegaFour

OddPerfect/OmegaFour.lean · 268 lines · 1 public theorem · imports Abundancy, Euler, Cyclotomic, Seed.Example

At least four distinct prime factors.

Proof idea. With exactly three prime factors the bound 2 < ∏ p/(p − 1) forces the support {3, 5, s} with s ∈ {7, 11, 13}. In each case some prime with even exponent has a component sum larger than 1 whose prime factors must lie among the other two primes; multiplicative orders, and in one case the extra prime 3221 or 61, rule out every candidate exponent.

An odd perfect number has at least four distinct prime factors.

OddPerfect.OmegaFive

OddPerfect/OmegaFive.lean · 515 lines · 1 public theorem · imports Certificate, OmegaFour, SmallestPrime, SpecialPrime, Zsigmondy

At least five distinct prime factors, closed by a kernel computation over 104 explicit supports.

Proof idea. With four prime factors, 3 divides N and the product bound leaves the supports {3, 5, r, s} with 17 ≤ r ≤ 31 and s < 256, {3, 7, r, s} with r ∈ {11, 13} and s ≤ 26, and the families {3, 5, 11, s} and {3, 5, 13, s}, cut to s ≤ 100 and s ≤ 46 ({3, 5, 7, s} is out since 105 does not divide N). That is 70 + 6 + 20 + 8 = 104 supports. For each, a Boolean function lists the candidate exponents of each prime (from Euler's form and primitive divisors: e = 1 for the special prime, or e + 1 dividing y − 1 for some y in the support) and checks that σ(xe) always has a prime factor outside the support and 2; decide +kernel evaluates it.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.five_le_card_primeFactors

theorem five_le_card_primeFactors {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) : 5 ≤ N.primeFactors.card

An odd perfect number has at least five distinct prime factors.

Proof idea. With four prime factors the product bound 2 < ∏ p/(p − 1) leaves 104 explicit supports. For each one a kernel computation lists the possible exponents of each prime and shows that some σ(xe) always has a prime factor outside the support.

OddPerfect.SmallestPrime

OddPerfect/SmallestPrime.lean · 282 lines · 9 public theorems · imports Abundancy

Bounds on the smallest prime factor p₁: p₁ ≤ ω and 3p₁ < 2ω + 6, and ω ≥ 7 without 3, ω ≥ 15 without 3 and 5.

Proof idea. Everything rests on 2 < ∏ p/(p − 1) over the prime factors. If all elements of a set are at least m, the product telescopes to at most 1 + |S|/(m − 1), giving p₁ ≤ ω; over odd numbers the squared product is at most 1 + 2|S|/(m − 2), giving 3p₁ < 2ω + 6. The bounds without 3 or 5 compare the product with an explicit set of small primes.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.prod_div_sub_one_le

theorem prod_div_sub_one_le (S : Finset ℕ) {m : ℕ} (hm : 2 ≤ m) (hS : ∀ s ∈ S, m ≤ s) :
  ∏ s ∈ S, (s : ℚ) / (s - 1) ≤ 1 + S.card / (m - 1)

If every element of a finite set S is at least m ≥ 2, then ∏ s ∈ S, s/(s-1) ≤ 1 + |S|/(m-1), the telescoped product over m, m+1, …, m+|S|-1.

OddPerfect.sq_prod_div_sub_one_le

  (hS : ∀ s ∈ S, m ≤ s ∧ Odd s) :
  (∏ s ∈ S, (s : ℚ) / (s - 1)) ^ 2 ≤ 1 + 2 * S.card / (m - 2)

If every element of a finite set S is odd and at least m ≥ 3, then (∏ s ∈ S, s/(s-1))^2 ≤ 1 + 2|S|/(m-2), from the telescoped product over m, m+2, ….

OddPerfect.prod_div_sub_one_mul_le_of_card_lt

theorem prod_div_sub_one_mul_le_of_card_lt {S T : Finset ℕ} {L : ℕ}
  (hT : ∀ t ∈ T, 2 ≤ t ∧ t ≤ L) (hS : ∀ s ∈ S, 2 ≤ s) (hST : ∀ s ∈ S, s ∉ T → L < s)
  (hcard : S.card < T.card) :
  (∏ s ∈ S, (s : ℚ) / (s - 1)) * ((L : ℚ) / (L - 1)) ≤ ∏ t ∈ T, (t : ℚ) / (t - 1)

Comparison with an explicit set T: if T consists of numbers in [2, L], every element of S is at least 2, every element of S outside T exceeds L, and |S| < |T|, then (∏ s ∈ S, s/(s-1)) * (L/(L-1)) ≤ ∏ t ∈ T, t/(t-1).

OddPerfect.three_mul_minFac_lt

theorem three_mul_minFac_lt {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  3 * N.minFac < 2 * N.primeFactors.card + 6

p₁ < (2/3) ω + 2.

Proof idea. The product of p/(p − 1) over the prime factors exceeds 2. Over odd numbers at least p₁, its square telescopes to at most 1 + 2ω/(p₁ − 2). So 4 < 1 + 2ω/(p₁ − 2), which rearranges to 3p₁ < 2ω + 6.

OddPerfect.Counting

OddPerfect/Counting.lean · 251 lines · 12 public theorems · imports Euler, Network, Seed.ThreeFree

Linear inequalities between Ω and ω: 2ω ≤ Ω + 1 and 12ω ≤ 5Ω + 6v₃(N) + 9.

Proof idea. Every ordinary exponent is even, hence at least 2, which gives 2ω ≤ Ω + 1. Exponent 2 primes ≡ 1 (mod 3) each spend one unit of v₃(N). For the set A₂ of exponent 2 primes not ≡ 1 (mod 3), every prime factor of σ(p²) is ≡ 1 (mod 3) and so outside A₂; conservation at those receivers gives |A₂| ≤ Ω − 2|A₂|. Combining the two counts gives the inequality.

OddPerfect.three_free_sq_transfer

theorem three_free_sq_transfer {N p t : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ N)
  (hp : p.Prime) (he : N.factorization p = 2) (ht : t.Prime) (hdiv : t ∣ σ 1 (p ^ 2)) :
  p % 3 = 2 ∧ t % 3 = 1 ∧ t ∣ N ∧ (Odd (N.factorization t) ∨ 4 ≤ N.factorization t)

Step 2b: if 3 ∤ N, an exponent 2 prime is 2 (mod 3), and each prime factor of its divisor sum is 1 (mod 3), divides N, and is either the special prime (odd exponent) or has exponent at least 4.

OddPerfect.three_mul_card_sq_mod_three_ne_one_le

  3 * {p ∈ N.primeFactors | N.factorization p = 2 ∧ p % 3 ≠ 1}.card ≤
    ∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, N.factorization p

Transfer bound: let A₂ be the exponent 2 primes not 1 (mod 3). For p ∈ A₂ every prime factor of σ(p ^ 2) is 1 (mod 3), hence outside A₂; conservation at the receiving primes gives |A₂| ≤ Ω - 2 |A₂|.

OddPerfect.CountingZelinsky

OddPerfect/CountingZelinsky.lean · 434 lines · 10 public theorems · imports Counting

8ω ≤ 3Ω + 7 when 3 does not divide N, and 21ω ≤ 8Ω + 39 in general, after Zelinsky (arXiv 1706.07009).

Proof idea. A ledger of the exponent 2 primes, split by residue modulo 3, with conservation of the 3-free parts of σ(p²). The new input over Counting is injectivity: when σ(p²) has at most two prime factors its largest prime factor determines p, because a prime shared by σ(a²) and σ(b²) with b < a is at most a; and when the 3-free part of σ(p²) is prime it determines p, using that 3(x² + x + 1) = y² + y + 1 has no solution with x an odd prime (it forces y = 3a + 1 and x(x + 1) = 3a(a + 1)). Linear arithmetic on the resulting relations gives both bounds.

OddPerfect.three_mul_phi3_ne_phi3

theorem three_mul_phi3_ne_phi3 {x y : ℕ} (hx : x.Prime) (hx2 : x ≠ 2) :
  3 * (1 + x + x ^ 2) ≠ 1 + y + y ^ 2

If x is an odd prime then 3 (x ^ 2 + x + 1) is never of the form y ^ 2 + y + 1.

OddPerfect.shared_prime_le

theorem shared_prime_le {a b P : ℕ} (ha : a % 3 = 2) (hb : b % 3 = 2) (hba : b < a)
  (hP : P.Prime) (hP3 : P % 3 = 1) (hPa : P ∣ 1 + a + a ^ 2) (hPb : P ∣ 1 + b + b ^ 2) :
  P ≤ a

If b < a are both 2 (mod 3) and a prime P ≡ 1 (mod 3) divides both a ^ 2 + a + 1 and b ^ 2 + b + 1, then P ≤ a.

OddPerfect.exists_prime_dvd_le_sq_of_cardFactors_le_two

theorem exists_prime_dvd_le_sq_of_cardFactors_le_two {n : ℕ} (hn : 2 ≤ n) (hΩ : Ω n ≤ 2) :
  ∃ P, P.Prime ∧ P ∣ n ∧ n ≤ P ^ 2

A number n ≥ 2 with at most two prime factors (with multiplicity) has a prime factor P with n ≤ P ^ 2.

OddPerfect.zelinsky_ledger

theorem zelinsky_ledger {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ a b x y W ρ F : ℕ,
    (∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, N.factorization p) = 2 * a + F + N.factorization 3 ∧
    N.primeFactors.card = a + ρ + (if 3 ∣ N then 1 else 0) ∧
    4 * ρ ≤ F + 3 + 2 * b ∧ W ≤ F ∧ 3 * a + 2 * b ≤ W + x + y ∧
    x ≤ a ∧ y ≤ x + b ∧ x ≤ ρ ∧ y ≤ ρ ∧ b ≤ N.factorization 3 ∧
    (3 ∣ N → 2 ≤ N.factorization 3)

With S₂ the exponent 2 primes that are 2 (mod 3) (a of them), S₁ those that are 1 (mod 3) (b of them), R the remaining prime factors other than 3 (ρ of them, total exponent F), W the number of prime factors with multiplicity of the 3-free parts of σ(p ^ 2) over p ∈ S₁ ∪ S₂, X the primes of S₂ whose σ(p ^ 2) has at most two prime factors, and Y the primes of S₁ ∪ S₂ whose 3-free part of σ(p ^ 2) is prime, the listed linear relations hold. The bounds x ≤ ρ and y ≤ ρ are the two injectivity statements (largest prime factor, and the 3-free part itself).

theorem twentyone_mul_card_le {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  21 * N.primeFactors.card ≤ 8 * (∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, N.factorization p) + 39

Both cases at once: 21 ω ≤ 8 Ω + 39 for every odd perfect number. In the 3-free case this follows from 8 ω ≤ 3 Ω + 7 and the Euler bound 2 ω ≤ Ω + 1.

OddPerfect.eight_mul_omega_le_of_not_three_dvd

theorem eight_mul_omega_le_of_not_three_dvd {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ N) :
  8 * ω N ≤ 3 * Ω N + 7

8 ω(N) ≤ 3 Ω(N) + 7 when 3 ∤ N, in terms of Ω and ω.

Proof idea. A ledger of the exponent 2 primes and their divisor sums σ(p²), with conservation at the receiving primes, plus two injections: the largest prime factor of σ(p²), and the 3-free part of σ(p²) when it is prime, each determine p.

OddPerfect.RadicalBound

OddPerfect/RadicalBound.lean · 662 lines · 18 public theorems · imports Abundancy, SmallestPrime, Euler, SpecialPrime

Identities linking abundancy to the radical R and to φ(R), with consequences such as 2φ(R) < R for perfect numbers and linear bounds on the second, third and fourth smallest prime factors.

Proof idea. Each component contributes σ(pe)/pe = (p/(p − 1))(1 − p−(e+1)), so the abundancy of N is H(N) = R/φ(R) times a product of factors below 1. For a perfect N the abundancy is 2, hence R/φ(R) > 2, and since R and 2φ(R) are integers, R − 2φ(R) ≥ 1, which gives H(N) − 2 ≥ 1/φ(R). Keeping one factor 1 − p−(e+1) and bounding the others by 1 gives the component bound R ≤ pe+1(R − 2φ(R)).

theorem abundancy_eq_prod_mul_prod {n : ℕ} (hn : n ≠ 0) :

    ∏ p ∈ n.primeFactors, (1 - ((p : ℚ) ^ (n.factorization p + 1))⁻¹)

OddPerfect.le_two_mul_prod_sub_one_sub_one

theorem le_two_mul_prod_sub_one_sub_one {S : Finset ℕ} (hS : ∀ p ∈ S, Odd p ∧ 1 < p)
  (hle : ∏ p ∈ S, (p : ℚ) / (p - 1) ≤ 2) {q : ℕ} (hq : 1 < q)
  (hgt : 2 < (∏ p ∈ S, (p : ℚ) / (p - 1)) * ((q : ℚ) / (q - 1))) :
  q ≤ 2 * ∏ p ∈ S, (p - 1) - 1

If S is a finite set of odd numbers greater than one (for instance distinct odd primes) with ∏_{p ∈ S} p/(p-1) ≤ 2, and q > 1 satisfies ∏_{p ∈ S} p/(p-1) * q/(q-1) > 2, then q ≤ 2 ∏_{p ∈ S} (p - 1) - 1.

OddPerfect.abundancy_three_components_lt

theorem abundancy_three_components_lt {N a b c : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (ha : a ∈ N.primeFactors)

For any three distinct prime factors a < b < c of an odd perfect number N, σ(a^e_a b^e_b c^e_c)/(a^e_a b^e_b c^e_c) < 255/128. In particular this holds for the three smallest prime factors.

OddPerfect.UpperBound

OddPerfect/UpperBound.lean · 773 lines · 6 public theorems · imports Abundancy

N < 24k for an odd perfect number with k distinct prime factors, from the general bound dN < (d + 1)4k for odd N with σ(N)/N = n/d.

Proof idea. The key lemma (cook_lemma in the source): for integers 2 ≤ x₁ < ⋯ < xr with ∏ (1 − 1/xi) ≤ a/b below the same product without xr, one has a∏ xi < (a + 1)2r, proved by strong induction on r with a majorization comparison against the extremal sequence (a + 1)2i + 1. The theorem then grows a set of known primes: the lemma is applied first to new primes and then to prime power components, which splits off a coprime factor V of N; the cofactor N/V is again odd with rational abundancy of denominator dσ(V), and the induction runs on the number of prime factors.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.UpperBound.cook_lemma

theorem cook_lemma {G : Finset ℕ} {a b m : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (h2 : ∀ x ∈ G, 2 ≤ x) (hm : m ∈ G)
  (hmax : ∀ x ∈ G, x ≤ m) (hle : ∏ x ∈ G, (1 - 1 / (x : ℚ)) ≤ a / b)
  (hlt : (a : ℚ) / b < ∏ x ∈ G.erase m, (1 - 1 / (x : ℚ))) :
  a * ∏ x ∈ G, x < (a + 1) ^ (2 ^ G.card)

Let G be a finset of integers x ≥ 2 with largest element m, and let a > 0. If ∏_{x ∈ G} (1 - 1/x) ≤ a/b < ∏_{x ∈ G, x ≠ m} (1 - 1/x) then a ∏_{x ∈ G} x < (a + 1)^(2^|G|).

OddPerfect.UpperBound.select

theorem select {F : Finset ℕ} {a b : ℕ} (ha : 0 < a) (h2 : ∀ x ∈ F, 2 ≤ x)
  (hF : ∏ x ∈ F, (1 - 1 / (x : ℚ)) ≤ a / b) :
  ∃ G ⊆ F, ∏ x ∈ G, (1 - 1 / (x : ℚ)) ≤ a / b ∧ a * ∏ x ∈ G, x < (a + 1) ^ (2 ^ G.card)

If ∏_{x ∈ F} (1 - 1/x) ≤ a/b for a finset F of integers x ≥ 2 and a > 0, then some initial segment G ⊆ F satisfies ∏_{x ∈ G} (1 - 1/x) ≤ a/b and a ∏_{x ∈ G} x < (a + 1)^(2^|G|).

OddPerfect.UpperBound.general

theorem general : ∀ (k N d n : ℕ) (S : Finset ℕ), N.primeFactors.card = k → Odd N → 0 < d →
  d * σ 1 N = n * N → S ⊆ N.primeFactors →
  d * N < (d * ∏ p ∈ S, p + 1) ^ (2 ^ (2 * k - S.card))

The form used for induction: if N is odd with k distinct prime factors, d σ(N) = n N with d > 0, and S is a set of prime factors of N, then d N < (d ∏_{p ∈ S} p + 1)^(2^(2k - |S|)).

An odd perfect number with k distinct prime factors is below 2^(4^k).

Proof idea. An induction on the number of prime factors, driven by a lemma on products of (1 − 1/x) over increasing integers: at each step a coprime factor V of N is split off and the cofactor still has rational abundancy, with a controlled denominator.

OddPerfect.Zsigmondy

OddPerfect/Zsigmondy.lean · 249 lines · 4 public theorems · imports Cyclotomic

Primitive prime divisors for bases a ≥ 3: for every n ≥ 3 some prime q has a of order exactly n modulo q.

Proof idea. Through the cyclotomic value Φn(a). A prime factor of Φn(a) not dividing n is primitive. Otherwise every prime factor q divides n, the order of a modulo q is the part of n prime to q, which forces a single such prime, and lifting the exponent shows it divides Φn(a) only once. Then Φn(a) = q, contradicting Φn(a) > (a − 1)φ(n) ≥ 2q−1 ≥ q. Consequence: each divisor d ≥ 3 of e + 1 yields a prime factor of N congruent to 1 modulo d.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.orderOf_eq_ordCompl_of_dvd_cyclotomic_eval

theorem orderOf_eq_ordCompl_of_dvd_cyclotomic_eval {a q n : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime) (hn : n ≠ 0)
  (hdiv : (q : ℤ) ∣ (cyclotomic n ℤ).eval (a : ℤ)) :
  orderOf (a : ZMod q) = ordCompl[q] n ∧ ordCompl[q] n ∣ q - 1

If a prime q divides the cyclotomic value Φ_n(a) with n ≠ 0, then the order of a modulo q is the part of n prime to q, and it divides q - 1.

For bases a ≥ 3 and n ≥ 3 there is a prime q such that a has multiplicative order exactly n modulo q.

  (hd3 : 3 ≤ d) (hd : d ∣ e + 1) :

OddPerfect.exists_primeFactor_modEq_one

theorem exists_primeFactor_modEq_one {N p d : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N)
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hd3 : 3 ≤ d) (hd : d ∣ N.factorization p + 1) :
  ∃ q ∈ N.primeFactors, orderOf (p : ZMod q) = d ∧ q % d = 1

OddPerfect.ExponentCap

OddPerfect/ExponentCap.lean · 298 lines · 15 public theorems · imports Zsigmondy, Euler, SpecialPrime, UpperBound

Exponents are capped by the largest prime factor (2e + 3 ≤ R), the divisors of e + 1 inject into the prime factors, and finiteness for fixed ω or bounded primes.

Proof idea. For an ordinary prime with exponent e, a primitive prime of order e + 1 divides σ(pe), hence N, and is ≡ 1 (mod 2(e + 1)), so it is at least 2e + 3. For the special prime with exponent k ≥ 5, the primitive primes of orders k + 1 and (k + 1)/2 are distinct and both ≡ 1 (mod k + 1); for k = 1 the special prime itself is at least 5. Distinct divisors d ≥ 3 of e + 1 give distinct primes since p has order exactly d at each. Finiteness for fixed ω uses the bound N < 24ω.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.exists_primeFactor_two_mul_factorization_add_three_le

theorem exists_primeFactor_two_mul_factorization_add_three_le (h : OddPerfect N) {p : ℕ}
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) : ∃ r ∈ N.primeFactors, 2 * N.factorization p + 3 ≤ r

For every prime factor p of an odd perfect number with exponent e, some prime factor is at least 2 e + 3. For an ordinary prime this is a primitive prime of order e + 1, which is ≡ 1 (mod 2 (e + 1)). For the special prime with exponent k ≥ 5 the primitive primes of orders k + 1 and (k + 1) / 2 are distinct and both ≡ 1 (mod k + 1). For k = 1 the special prime itself is ≡ 1 (mod 4), hence at least 5.

OddPerfect.two_mul_factorization_add_three_le

theorem two_mul_factorization_add_three_le (h : OddPerfect N) {p R : ℕ}
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hR : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ R) :
  2 * N.factorization p + 3 ≤ R

Row length, sharp form: if every prime factor of an odd perfect number is at most R, then every exponent e satisfies 2 e + 3 ≤ R.

Proof idea. For an ordinary prime with exponent e, a primitive prime of order e + 1 divides σ(pe) and hence N, and it is ≡ 1 (mod 2(e + 1)), so it is at least 2e + 3. The special prime is handled with two primitive primes, of orders k + 1 and (k + 1)/2. Example: σ(3⁴) = 11², and 3 has order 5 modulo 11 = 2·4 + 3.

  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hR : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ R) :
  N.factorization p + 2 ≤ R

Row length: if every prime factor of an odd perfect number is at most R, then every exponent e satisfies e + 2 ≤ R.

Fan bound for an ordinary prime: if the exponent e is even, then e + 1 has at most ω(N) divisors.

OddPerfect.card_primeFactors_le

theorem card_primeFactors_le (h : OddPerfect N) {R : ℕ} (hR : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ R) :
  N.primeFactors.card ≤ R - 2

If every prime factor of an odd perfect number is at most R, then ω(N) ≤ R - 2, since the prime factors are distinct odd primes in [3, R].

  (hR : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ R) : N ≤ R ^ ((R - 2) * N.primeFactors.card)

If every prime factor of an odd perfect number is at most R, then N ≤ R ^ ((R - 2) * ω(N)): each component p ^ e is at most R ^ (R - 2).

OddPerfect.ValuationBounds

OddPerfect/ValuationBounds.lean · 377 lines · 9 public theorems · imports Valuation, ExponentCap contains novelty unverified

Bounds on the exponent of a prime q in terms of ω(N): the q-adic ledger bounded term by term, with special forms for primes q with q − 1 a power of two and for q = 3. The main formula factorization_le_sq_add_sum_orderWeight is an analogue of a corollary of Thackeray, proved here directly and under different hypotheses: his statement concerns odd squares.

Proof idea. The q-adic ledger vq(N) = Σ vq(σ(pep)) is bounded term by term. A component with p ≡ 1 (mod q) contributes vq(ep + 1), and q, q², …, qj are distinct divisors at least 3 of ep + 1, so the injection of divisors into prime factors gives vq(ep + 1) ≤ ω − 1. A component of order d > 1 contributes vq(pd − 1) + vq((ep + 1)/d), and the second summand is bounded the same way.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.sum_padicValNat_sigma_le_factorization

theorem sum_padicValNat_sigma_le_factorization (hN : N.Perfect) {q : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime)
  (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (S : Finset ℕ) :
  ∑ p ∈ S, padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) ≤ N.factorization q

Budget overshoot, lower bound form: for a perfect number N and an odd prime q, the components over any finite set S of primes supply at most v_q(N). Primes outside N.primeFactors contribute zero.

OddPerfect.sum_padicValNat_two_sigma_le_one

theorem sum_padicValNat_two_sigma_le_one (h : OddPerfect N) (S : Finset ℕ) :
  ∑ p ∈ S, padicValNat 2 (σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) ≤ 1

Budget overshoot at 2: for an odd perfect number the components over any finite set supply at most one factor of 2.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_dvd_succ_le

theorem padicValNat_dvd_succ_le (h : OddPerfect N) {p q n : ℕ} (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors)
  (hq : q.Prime) (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (hn : n ∣ N.factorization p + 1) :
  padicValNat q n ≤ N.primeFactors.card - 1

For a prime factor p of an odd perfect number, every divisor of v_p(N) + 1 has q-adic valuation at most ω(N) - 1, for every odd prime q.

OddPerfect.padicValNat_sigma_le_card_sub_one_add

theorem padicValNat_sigma_le_card_sub_one_add (h : OddPerfect N) {p q : ℕ}
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hq : q.Prime) (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (hpq : p ≠ q) :
  padicValNat q (σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) ≤
    (N.primeFactors.card - 1) + orderWeight q p (N.factorization p)

Per component bound: v_q(σ(p ^ e)) ≤ (ω(N) - 1) + orderWeight q p e.

OddPerfect.factorization_le_sq_add_sum_orderWeightnovelty unverified

theorem factorization_le_sq_add_sum_orderWeight (h : OddPerfect N) {q : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime) :
  N.factorization q ≤ (N.primeFactors.card - 1) ^ 2 +
    ∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors.erase q, orderWeight q p (N.factorization p)

For every prime q, v_q(N) ≤ (ω(N) - 1) ^ 2 + ∑_{p ≠ q} orderWeight q p v_p(N).

OddPerfect.mod_eq_one_of_dvd_sigma_of_even

theorem mod_eq_one_of_dvd_sigma_of_even {p q a j : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hqj : q - 1 = 2 ^ j) (ha : Even a) (hdvd : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ a)) : p % q = 1

If q - 1 is a power of two and q ∣ σ(p ^ a) with a even, then p ≡ 1 (mod q). The order of p modulo q divides q - 1, so an order above 1 is even and cannot divide the odd number a + 1.

OddPerfect.factorization_le_of_fermat

theorem factorization_le_of_fermat (h : OddPerfect N) {q j s : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime)
  (hqj : q - 1 = 2 ^ j) (hs : s.Prime) (hodd : Odd (N.factorization s)) :
  N.factorization q ≤ (N.primeFactors.card - 1) ^ 2 + padicValNat q (s + 1)

If q is prime with q - 1 a power of two and s is the special prime of the odd perfect number N, then v_q(N) ≤ (ω(N) - 1) ^ 2 + v_q(s + 1). The last term vanishes unless s ≡ -1 (mod q).

OddPerfect.factorization_three_le

theorem factorization_three_le (h : OddPerfect N) {s : ℕ} (hs : s.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization s)) :
  N.factorization 3 ≤ (N.primeFactors.card - 1) ^ 2 + padicValNat 3 (s + 1)

The prime 3: v_3(N) ≤ (ω(N) - 1) ^ 2 + v_3(s + 1) for the special prime s; the last term is 0 unless s ≡ 2 (mod 3).

OddPerfect.three_mul_le_of_dvd_sigma

theorem three_mul_le_of_dvd_sigma {p q k m : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hp2 : p ≠ 2)
  (hq2 : q ≠ 2) (hdvd : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ k)) (hm : q ^ m ∣ p + 1) : 3 * m ≤ k

If p and q are odd primes with p ∣ σ(q ^ k) and q ^ m ∣ p + 1, then k ≥ 3 m. Both oddness hypotheses are needed: (p, q, k, m) = (2, 3, 1, 1) and (7, 2, 2, 3) violate the conclusion.

OddPerfect.AttackPrimality

OddPerfect/AttackPrimality.lean · 344 lines · 12 public theorems · imports Network, Valuation, Zsigmondy, SpecialPrime, Spoof

Order data on the edges of the network: an edge from a smaller prime to a larger one has order at least 3, and if the largest prime factor P has even exponent e then 2e + 5 ≤ P. The decidable statement MaxReceives asks for a divisor d ≥ 3 of e + 1 with bd ≡ 1 (mod P); it does not require d to be the exact order. Descartes' spoof violates it, while every odd perfect number satisfies it.

Proof idea. For an edge p → r the order d of p modulo r divides both ep + 1 and r − 1. Orders 1 and 2 would force r to divide p − 1 or p + 1, which is impossible for an odd prime r > p, so an upward edge has d ≥ 3. By conservation the largest prime factor receives an edge, necessarily upward.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.three_le_orderOf_of_lt

theorem three_le_orderOf_of_lt {p r e : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hp2 : p ≠ 2) (hr : r.Prime)
  (hpr : p < r) (hd : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) :
  3 ≤ orderOf (p : ZMod r) ∧ orderOf (p : ZMod r) ∣ e + 1 ∧
    r % orderOf (p : ZMod r) = 1

Upward edges have order at least three. If p < r are primes, p odd, and r ∣ σ(p ^ e), then the order d of p modulo r satisfies 3 ≤ d, d ∣ e + 1 and r % d = 1.

OddPerfect.odd_orderOf_of_even

theorem odd_orderOf_of_even {p r e : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hr : r.Prime) (hpr : p ≠ r)
  (he : Even e) (hd : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) : Odd (orderOf (p : ZMod r))

A sender with even exponent has odd order at every receiver: if p ≠ r are primes, e is even and r ∣ σ(p ^ e), then the order of p modulo r is odd.

OddPerfect.eq_one_of_fermat_receiver

theorem eq_one_of_fermat_receiver {p r e j : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hr : r.Prime)
  (hrj : r - 1 = 2 ^ j) (hpr : p ≠ r) (he : Even e) (hd : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ e)) :
  (p : ZMod r) = 1 ∧ r ∣ e + 1

If r is a prime with r - 1 = 2 ^ j, p ≠ r is a prime, e is even and r ∣ σ(p ^ e), then p ≡ 1 (mod r) and r ∣ e + 1. The hypothesis that e is even is needed: 17 ∣ σ(67) = 68 while 67 ≡ -1 (mod 17), with exponent 1.

OddPerfect.upward_edge

theorem upward_edge (h : OddPerfect N) {p r : ℕ} (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors)
  (hr : r ∈ N.primeFactors) (hpr : p < r) (hd : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) :
  3 ≤ orderOf (p : ZMod r) ∧ orderOf (p : ZMod r) ∣ N.factorization p + 1 ∧
    r % orderOf (p : ZMod r) = 1 ∧ 2 ≤ N.factorization p

Upward edges of an odd perfect number. If p < r are prime factors of N and r ∣ σ(p ^ e_p), then the order d of p modulo r satisfies 3 ≤ d, d ∣ e_p + 1, r ≡ 1 (mod d), and e_p ≥ 2.

OddPerfect.upward_edge_of_even

theorem upward_edge_of_even (h : OddPerfect N) {p r : ℕ} (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors)
  (hr : r ∈ N.primeFactors) (hpr : p < r) (hd : r ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p))
  (he : Even (N.factorization p)) :
  Odd (orderOf (p : ZMod r)) ∧ r % (2 * orderOf (p : ZMod r)) = 1 ∧
    2 * orderOf (p : ZMod r) + 1 ≤ r

Upward edges from an ordinary prime (even exponent): the order d is odd and r ≡ 1 (mod 2 d), so 2 d + 1 ≤ r.

OddPerfect.exists_upward_edge_into_largest

theorem exists_upward_edge_into_largest (h : OddPerfect N) {P : ℕ} (hP : P ∈ N.primeFactors)
  (hmax : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ P) :
  ∃ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p < P ∧ P ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p) ∧
    3 ≤ orderOf (p : ZMod P) ∧ orderOf (p : ZMod P) ∣ N.factorization p + 1 ∧
    P % orderOf (p : ZMod P) = 1 ∧ 2 ≤ N.factorization p

The largest prime factor P of an odd perfect number receives an upward edge: some prime factor p < P has P ∣ σ(p ^ e_p), and the order d of p modulo P satisfies 3 ≤ d, d ∣ e_p + 1, P ≡ 1 (mod d); in particular e_p ≥ 2.

OddPerfect.two_le_factorization_of_dvd_largest

theorem two_le_factorization_of_dvd_largest (h : OddPerfect N) {P p : ℕ}
  (hP : P ∈ N.primeFactors) (hmax : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ P) (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors)
  (hd : P ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) : 2 ≤ N.factorization p

No prime with exponent one (that is, the special prime with exponent 1) feeds the largest prime factor: every component p with P ∣ σ(p ^ e_p) has e_p ≥ 2.

OddPerfect.two_mul_factorization_add_five_le_largest

theorem two_mul_factorization_add_five_le_largest (h : OddPerfect N) {P : ℕ}
  (hP : P ∈ N.primeFactors) (hmax : ∀ r ∈ N.primeFactors, r ≤ P)
  (he : Even (N.factorization P)) : 2 * N.factorization P + 5 ≤ P

Row bound at the top. If the largest prime factor P of an odd perfect number has even exponent e, then 2 e + 5 ≤ P. The primitive prime of order e + 1 for P is a prime factor r ≡ 1 (mod 2 (e + 1)) with r < P.

OddPerfect.special_receiver_dichotomy

theorem special_receiver_dichotomy (h : OddPerfect N) {q p : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hpq : p ≠ q)
  (hd : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) :
  ((p : ZMod q) = 1 ∧ q ∣ N.factorization p + 1) ∨
    (3 ≤ orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∧ Odd (orderOf (p : ZMod q)) ∧
      orderOf (p : ZMod q) ∣ N.factorization p + 1 ∧ q % (4 * orderOf (p : ZMod q)) = 1)

Receivers of the special prime. Let q be the special prime (odd exponent) of an odd perfect number and p ≠ q a prime factor with q ∣ σ(p ^ e_p). Then either p ≡ 1 (mod q) and q ∣ e_p + 1, or the order d of p modulo q is odd, d ≥ 3, d ∣ e_p + 1 and q ≡ 1 (mod 4 d).

  MaxReceives N.primeFactors N.factorization

Every odd perfect number satisfies MaxReceives on its prime factors and exponents.

OddPerfect.not_maxReceives_descartes

theorem not_maxReceives_descartes : ¬ MaxReceives {3, 7, 11, 13, 22021} descartesExp

Descartes' spoof violates MaxReceives: no base divides geom b (e b) by 22021. The cause is that 22021 = 19² · 61 is not prime, and the formal divisor sums deliver its factors 19, 19, 61 from three different components (7², 11², 13²).

OddPerfect.descartes_data

theorem descartes_data :
  Spoof.descartes.map Prod.fst = [3, 7, 11, 13, 22021] ∧
    ∀ x ∈ Spoof.descartes, descartesExp x.1 = x.2

descartesExp and the Finset {3, 7, 11, 13, 22021} are exactly the data of Spoof.descartes.

OddPerfect.Phi3Algebra

OddPerfect/Phi3Algebra.lean · 1086 lines · 43 public theorems · imports Phi3

Reusable algebra of φ₃(x) = x² + x + 1, and of x⁴ + x³ + x² + x + 1, for the inequalities between Ω and ω: factorization identities such as φ₃(b²) = φ₃(b)(b² − b + 1) and φ₃((a + 1)²) = φ₃(a)φ₃(a + 1), the solutions of φ₃(x) = m·φ₃(b) when φ₃(b) is prime, and the fact that for a prime c at most two residues x modulo c have c dividing x² + x + 1.

Proof idea. The identities are polynomial identities checked by ring after casting to the integers. For the equation φ₃(x) = m·φ₃(b) with p = φ₃(b) prime and 1 < m ≤ p: x² < φ₃(x) ≤ p² gives x < p, and x ≠ b because m > 1. Then x and b are two distinct roots of t² + t + 1 modulo p below p, and two such roots add up to p − 1, so x + b + 1 = b² + b + 1 and x = b². The counting statement follows because a quadratic has at most two roots modulo a prime.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.phi3_sq_eq

theorem phi3_sq_eq (b : ℕ) : phi3 (b ^ 2) = phi3 b * (b ^ 2 - b + 1)

The identity F(b ^ 2) = F(b) · (b ^ 2 - b + 1), where F = phi3. Natural subtraction is harmless here since b ≤ b ^ 2.

OddPerfect.phi3_eq_mul_phi3_prime

theorem phi3_eq_mul_phi3_prime {x b m : ℕ} (hp : (phi3 b).Prime)
  (h : phi3 x = m * phi3 b) (hm1 : 1 < m) (hmp : m ≤ phi3 b) :
  x = b ^ 2 ∧ m = b ^ 2 - b + 1

If F(b) is prime and F(x) = m · F(b) with 1 < m ≤ F(b), then x = b ^ 2 and m = b ^ 2 - b + 1.

OddPerfect.phi3_mod

theorem phi3_mod (z m : ℕ) : phi3 z % m = phi3 (z % m) % m

Reduction of phi3 modulo any m.

OddPerfect.phi3_common_prime_cases

theorem phi3_common_prime_cases {p x y : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hx : p ∣ phi3 x)
  (hy : p ∣ phi3 y) : x ≡ y [MOD p] ∨ p ∣ x + y + 1

Two inputs with a common prime divisor of phi3 are congruent modulo it, or their sum plus one is divisible by it (the natural number form of phi3_two_roots).

OddPerfect.ch_factorization1

theorem ch_factorization1 {a b c d e : ℕ} (hc : c.Prime) (ha : phi3 a = c * d)
  (hb : phi3 b = c * e) (hcd : d < c) (hde : e < d) :
  c = a + b + 1 ∧ a + e = b + d

If c is prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = c d, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = c e and c > d > e, then c = a + b + 1 and a - b = d - e (stated additively as a + e = b + d). The paper's positivity hypotheses are not needed. Compared with phi3_roots_sum, the size condition a, b < c is derived from c > d > e, and the cofactor relation is added.

OddPerfect.ch_zelProof1

theorem ch_zelProof1 {a b c d : ℕ} (hc : c.Prime) (ha : phi3 a = c * d) (hb : phi3 b = c)
  (hcd : d < c) (hd : 1 < d) : a = b ^ 2

If c is prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = c d, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = c and c > d > 1, then a = b ^ 2.

OddPerfect.ch_zelProof1_not_prime

theorem ch_zelProof1_not_prime {a b c d : ℕ} (hc : c.Prime) (ha : phi3 a = c * d)
  (hb : phi3 b = c) (hcd : d < c) (hd : 1 < d) : ¬ a.Prime

Under the same hypotheses a is not prime.

OddPerfect.ch_zelProof2

theorem ch_zelProof2 {a b c d f : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hb : b.Prime) (hc : c.Prime)
  (hd : d.Prime) (hf : f.Prime) (ha3 : 3 < a) (hb3 : 3 < b) (hc3 : 3 < c) (hd3 : 3 < d)
  (hf3 : 3 < f) (hcd : d < c) (hdf : f < d) :
  ¬ (phi3 a = c * d ∧ phi3 b = c * f)

For primes a, b, c, d, f > 3 with c > d > f, it is impossible that a ^ 2 + a + 1 = c d and b ^ 2 + b + 1 = c f. So two primes of S_{2,2} never share their largest contributed prime.

OddPerfect.phi3_succ_sq

theorem phi3_succ_sq (a : ℕ) : phi3 ((a + 1) ^ 2) = phi3 a * phi3 (a + 1)

F((a + 1) ^ 2) = F(a) F(a + 1) for every a.

OddPerfect.hansen_nielsen_two

theorem hansen_nielsen_two {x a b : ℕ} (ha : 1 ≤ a) (hab : a ≤ b) (hp : (phi3 b).Prime) :
  phi3 x = phi3 a * phi3 b ↔ b = a + 1 ∧ x = (a + 1) ^ 2

For 1 ≤ a ≤ b with b ^ 2 + b + 1 prime, x ^ 2 + x + 1 = (a ^ 2 + a + 1)(b ^ 2 + b + 1) holds exactly when b = a + 1 and x = (a + 1) ^ 2. Only the larger factor needs to be prime, and the proof uses no arithmetic of ℤ[ζ₆].

OddPerfect.no_double_threat

theorem no_double_threat {x a b : ℕ} (hx : x.Prime) (ha : (phi3 a).Prime)
  (hb : (phi3 b).Prime) : phi3 x ≠ phi3 a * phi3 b

No "double threat": if x is prime then x ^ 2 + x + 1 is not the product of two primes of the form a ^ 2 + a + 1.

OddPerfect.phi3_eq_three_mul_phi3_prime

  (h : phi3 x = 3 * phi3 b) : b = 2 ∧ x = 4

If b ^ 2 + b + 1 is prime and x ^ 2 + x + 1 = 3 (b ^ 2 + b + 1), then b = 2 and x = 4.

There is no prime x with x ^ 2 + x + 1 = 3 (a ^ 2 + a + 1) and a ^ 2 + a + 1 prime (the hypotheses that a is prime and the primes are odd are not needed).

OddPerfect.card_le_two_of_dvd_phi3

For a prime c, at most two natural numbers below c have c ∣ x ^ 2 + x + 1.

OddPerfect.card_filter_range_dvd_phi3_le_two

OddPerfect.card_image_zmod_le_two_of_dvd_phi3

Without a size restriction: the inputs x with c ∣ x ^ 2 + x + 1, for a prime c, fall into at most two residue classes modulo c.

OddPerfect.zelinsky_shared_prime_two

theorem zelinsky_shared_prime_two {a b p : ℕ} (hab : a ≠ b) (ha2 : a % 2 = 1)
  (hb2 : b % 2 = 1) (ha3 : a % 3 = 2) (hb3 : b % 3 = 2) (hp : p.Prime)
  (hpa : p ∣ phi3 a) (hpb : p ∣ phi3 b) : 5 * p ≤ a + b + 1

If a ≠ b are odd, a ≡ b ≡ 2 (mod 3), and a prime p divides both a ^ 2 + a + 1 and b ^ 2 + b + 1, then 5 p ≤ a + b + 1. (The paper assumes a, b prime; only oddness is used.)

theorem zelinsky_shared_prime_one {a b p : ℕ} (hab : a ≠ b) (ha2 : a % 2 = 1)

If a ≠ b are odd, a ≡ b ≡ 1 (mod 3), and a prime p divides both a ^ 2 + a + 1 and b ^ 2 + b + 1, then 3 p ≤ a + b + 1.

OddPerfect.zelinsky_precursor

theorem zelinsky_precursor {a c : ℕ} (ha : a % 2 = 1) (hc : c.Prime) (hc2 : c % 2 = 1)
  (hp : (phi3 a).Prime) (hac : a ≠ c) (hd : phi3 a ∣ phi3 c) : 2 * phi3 a < c

If a ≠ c are odd, c is prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 is prime and divides c ^ 2 + c + 1, then 2 (a ^ 2 + a + 1) < c. (The paper assumes a prime; only oddness is used.)

OddPerfect.zelinsky_precursor_of_not_three_dvd

theorem zelinsky_precursor_of_not_three_dvd {a c : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (ha2' : a ≠ 2)
  (ha3 : a ≠ 3) (hc : c.Prime) (hc2' : c ≠ 2) (hp : (phi3 a).Prime) (hac : a ≠ c)
  (hd : phi3 a ∣ phi3 c) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ phi3 c) : 9 * phi3 a < 2 * c

If a ≠ c are odd primes (a, c ≠ 2), a ≠ 3, a ^ 2 + a + 1 is prime and divides c ^ 2 + c + 1, and 3 ∤ c ^ 2 + c + 1, then 9 (a ^ 2 + a + 1) < 2 c. The paper omits a ≠ 3, and then the statement is false: a = 3, c = 29, 29 ^ 2 + 29 + 1 = 13 · 67.

OddPerfect.no_phi3_eq_three_mul_phi3_mul_phi3

theorem no_phi3_eq_three_mul_phi3_mul_phi3 {a b c : ℕ} (ha : a % 2 = 1) (hb : b % 2 = 1)
  (hc : c.Prime) (hc2 : c % 2 = 1) (hpa : (phi3 a).Prime) (hpb : (phi3 b).Prime) :
  phi3 c ≠ 3 * phi3 a * phi3 b

There are no odd primes a, b, c with a ^ 2 + a + 1, b ^ 2 + b + 1 prime and c ^ 2 + c + 1 = 3 (a ^ 2 + a + 1)(b ^ 2 + b + 1). (Only oddness of a, b is used.)

OddPerfect.phi3_ne_cube_of_prime

theorem phi3_ne_cube_of_prime {x : ℕ} (hx : x.Prime) (k : ℕ) : phi3 x ≠ k ^ 3

If x is prime then x ^ 2 + x + 1 is not a perfect cube. (Primality matters: 18 ^ 2 + 18 + 1 = 7 ^ 3.)

OddPerfect.no_phi3_eq_triple_mod_five

theorem no_phi3_eq_triple_mod_five {a b c d : ℕ} (ha : a % 5 = 1) (hb : b % 5 = 1)
  (hc : c % 5 = 1) (hd : d % 5 = 1) : phi3 a ≠ phi3 b * phi3 c * phi3 d

There are no integers a, b, c, d, all ≡ 1 (mod 5), with a ^ 2 + a + 1 = (b ^ 2 + b + 1)(c ^ 2 + c + 1)(d ^ 2 + d + 1) (the left side is 3 and the right side 27 ≡ 2 modulo 5).

OddPerfect.hansen_nielsen_three_family

theorem hansen_nielsen_three_family {a b c x : ℕ} (hc : c * (a + b + 1) = a * b)
  (hx : x = c * (a * b + a + b) + a + b) : phi3 x = phi3 a * phi3 b * phi3 c

If c (a + b + 1) = a b and x = c (a b + a + b) + a + b, then x ^ 2 + x + 1 = (a ^ 2 + a + 1)(b ^ 2 + b + 1)(c ^ 2 + c + 1).

OddPerfect.hansen_nielsen_three_sporadic

theorem hansen_nielsen_three_sporadic :
  phi3 18 = phi3 2 * phi3 2 * phi3 2 ∧ phi3 25 = phi3 1 * phi3 2 * phi3 5 ∧
    phi3 22 = phi3 1 * phi3 3 * phi3 3

F(18) = F(2) ^ 3, F(25) = F(1) F(2) F(5), F(22) = F(1) F(3) ^ 2.

OddPerfect.quadruple_threat_191

    (phi3 3).Prime ∧ (phi3 5).Prime

191 ^ 2 + 191 + 1 = F(2) F(3) F(3) F(5) = 7 · 13 · 13 · 31, with 191 and the four factors prime.

For z ≡ 1 (mod 5), Φ₅(z) ≡ 5 (mod 25) (from the block identity: 5 ∣ z - 1 and 5 ∣ z ^ 3 + 2 z ^ 2 + 3 z + 4).

The exact power of 5 in Φ₅(z) for every natural z: it is 1 if z ≡ 1 (mod 5) and 0 otherwise.

OddPerfect.phi3_phi5

theorem phi3_phi5 (x : ℕ) :
  phi3 (phi5 x) =
    (x ^ 2 - x + 1) * (x ^ 6 + 3 * x ^ 5 + 5 * x ^ 4 + 6 * x ^ 3 + 7 * x ^ 2 + 6 * x + 3)

Φ₃(Φ₅(x)) = (x ^ 2 - x + 1)(x ^ 6 + 3 x ^ 5 + 5 x ^ 4 + 6 x ^ 3 + 7 x ^ 2 + 6 x + 3). Natural subtraction is harmless since x ≤ x ^ 2.

OddPerfect.gcd_phi3_phi5_factors_dvd

theorem gcd_phi3_phi5_factors_dvd (x : ℕ) :
  Nat.gcd (x ^ 2 - x + 1) (x ^ 6 + 3 * x ^ 5 + 5 * x ^ 4 + 6 * x ^ 3 + 7 * x ^ 2 + 6 * x + 3)
    ∣ 31

The gcd of the two factors in phi3_phi5 divides 31, via the explicit combination (5x⁵ + 21x⁴ + 44x³ + 58x² + 55x + 34) A - (5x + 1) B = 31.

OddPerfect.prime_dvd_phi3_phi5_factors

OddPerfect.ch_factorization3

theorem ch_factorization3 {a b d e f : ℕ} (hd : d.Prime) (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e)
  (hB : phi3 b = 3 * d * f) (hde : e ≤ d) (hef : f < e) :
  3 * d = a + b + 1 ∧ a + f = b + e

If d is prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 d f and d ≥ e > f, then 3 d = a + b + 1 and a - b = e - f (stated as a + f = b + e). The paper also assumes a, b prime; this is not needed.

OddPerfect.ch_factorization2

theorem ch_factorization2 {a b d e f : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hb : b.Prime) (hd : d.Prime)
  (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e) (hB : phi3 b = d * f) (hde : e ≤ d) (hef : f < e) :
  d = a + b + 1 ∧ a + f = b + 3 * e

If a, b, d are prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = d f and d ≥ e > f, then d = a + b + 1 and a - b = 3 e - f (stated as a + f = b + 3 e). The paper assumes d > e; d ≥ e suffices. The paper's mod 3 step tacitly assumes b ≡ 2 (mod 3); the cases b = 3 and b ≡ 1 (mod 3) are handled here separately (the latter by a size argument).

OddPerfect.ch_unique_S1_S31

theorem ch_unique_S1_S31 {a b d e : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hb : b.Prime) (hd : d.Prime)
  (he : 1 < e) (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e) (hB : phi3 b = d) (hde : e ≤ d) : False

There are no primes a, b, d and e > 1 with a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = d and d ≥ e.

OddPerfect.ch_unique_S21_S31

theorem ch_unique_S21_S31 {a b d e : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hd : d.Prime) (he : 1 < e)
  (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e) (hB : phi3 b = 3 * d) (hde : e ≤ d) : False

There are no primes a, d and e > 1, and no b, with a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 d and d ≥ e. (The paper writes d = a + b + 1 where 3 d = a + b + 1 is meant.)

OddPerfect.ch_semilinked_S31

theorem ch_semilinked_S31 {a b c d e f g : ℕ} (hd : d.Prime) (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e)
  (hB : phi3 b = 3 * d * f) (hC : phi3 c = 3 * d * g) (hde : e ≤ d) (hef : f < e)
  (hfg : g < f) : False

For a prime d there are no a, b, c and d ≥ e > f > g with a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 d f and c ^ 2 + c + 1 = 3 d g. (The paper's other primality and distinctness hypotheses are not needed.)

OddPerfect.yamada_gap

theorem yamada_gap {l q x₁ x₂ : ℕ} (hl : Odd l) (hq : q.Prime)
  (h1 : (x₁ : ZMod q) ^ l = 1) (h2 : (x₂ : ZMod q) ^ l = 1)
  (hq1 : x₁ ^ ((l - 1) / 2) < q)
  (hind : ∀ i j : ℕ, x₁ ^ i = x₂ ^ j → i = 0 ∧ j = 0) :
  x₁ ^ ((l + 1) / 6) < x₂

Let q be prime, let x₁, x₂ satisfy x₁ ^ l ≡ x₂ ^ l ≡ 1 (mod q), let x₁ and x₂ be multiplicatively independent (x₁ ^ i = x₂ ^ j only for i = j = 0), and let x₁ ^ ((l - 1) / 2) < q. Then x₂ > x₁ ^ ⌊(l + 1) / 6⌋. In the paper q is the common large prime of Φ_l(x₁) = p ^ {m₁} q and Φ_l(x₂) = p ^ {m₂} q with p ^ {m₁} < q, which gives the size hypothesis. For l = 3 the conclusion is only x₂ > 1; for l = 5 it is x₂ > x₁. The proof counts the l + 1 or more distinct residues x₁ ^ i x₂ ^ j < q that are l-th roots of unity modulo q.

OddPerfect.ch_unique_S1_S2

theorem ch_unique_S1_S2 {a b c d g h : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hb : b.Prime) (hc : c.Prime)
  (hd : d.Prime) (hb2 : b ≠ 2) (hd2 : d ≠ 2) (hd3 : d ≠ 3) (hcd : d < c)
  (hA : phi3 a = c * d) (hB : phi3 b = 3 * c) (hg : g.Prime) (hg2 : g ≠ 2)
  (hG : phi3 g = d * h) (hdh : h < d) (hh : h = 1 ∨ (h.Prime ∧ h ≠ 2)) : False

Let a, b, c, d be odd primes with c > d, d ≠ 3, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = c d and b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 c. Then there is no odd prime g with g ^ 2 + g + 1 = d h, d > h, where h = 1 or h is an odd prime.

OddPerfect.LargePrimes

OddPerfect/LargePrimes.lean · 614 lines · 18 public theorems · imports SpecialPrime, Network, Phi3 contains novelty unverified

Upper bounds for prime factors in terms of N. Every prime factor p satisfies p³ < 3N, and p⁴ < 2N when its exponent is at least 2; any two prime factors p < q satisfy p⁴q < 2N; every prime factor other than the largest satisfies p⁵ < 2N; and any two distinct prime factors satisfy (pq)⁴ < 6N². Along the way: no two odd primes satisfy both q | p² + p + 1 and p | q + 1, while 13 divides 3² + 3 + 1 and 3 divides 13² + 13 + 1.

Proof idea. The basic tool: if p has exact exponent e in N, then pe and σ(pe) are coprime divisors of 2N, so their product divides 2N, and pe < σ(pe) gives p2e < 2N. With e ≥ 2 this is p⁴ < 2N. Bounds involving two primes use that the divisor sums of two distinct components together divide 2N, and add a further prime factor q to the product whenever q does not divide σ(pe). For a prime with exponent 1, the cubic bound takes a component rc whose divisor sum is a multiple of p and compares its size with the part of p + 1 that it shares with rc.

OddPerfect.eq_one_or_two_or_four_of_dvd_phi3_of_dvd_succ

theorem eq_one_or_two_or_four_of_dvd_phi3_of_dvd_succ {p q : ℕ} (h1 : q ∣ p ^ 2 + p + 1)
  (h2 : p ∣ q + 1) : p = 1 ∨ p = 2 ∨ p = 4

For all natural numbers: if q ∣ p ^ 2 + p + 1 and p ∣ q + 1, then p ∈ {1, 2, 4}. The complete solution set is (1, 1), (1, 3), (2, 1), (2, 7), (4, 3), (4, 7).

OddPerfect.prime_dvd_phi3_dvd_succ

theorem prime_dvd_phi3_dvd_succ {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (h1 : q ∣ phi3 p) (h2 : p ∣ q + 1) : p = 2 ∧ q = 7

For primes, the only solution of q ∣ p ^ 2 + p + 1 and p ∣ q + 1 is (p, q) = (2, 7). So there is no such pair of odd primes (no σ_{1,2} two cycle in an odd perfect number).

OddPerfect.three_thirteen_two_cycle

theorem three_thirteen_two_cycle : 13 ∣ phi3 3 ∧ 3 ∣ phi3 13

The classical σ_{2,2} two cycle: 13 ∣ 3 ^ 2 + 3 + 1 and 3 ∣ 13 ^ 2 + 13 + 1. So the analogue of prime_dvd_phi3_dvd_succ with both exponents two is false.

OddPerfect.prime_two_cycle_succ

theorem prime_two_cycle_succ {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hpq : p ≠ q)

The σ_{1,1} two cycle: distinct primes with p ∣ q + 1 and q ∣ p + 1 are 2 and 3.

OddPerfect.phi6_two_cycle

The Φ6 two cycle: positive integers with x ∣ y ^ 2 - y + 1 and y ∣ x ^ 2 - x + 1 are x = y = 1. In contrast the Φ3 version has the infinite family (1, 1), (1, 3), (3, 13), (13, 61), ....

OddPerfect.pow_four_mul_lt_of_two_one

OddPerfect.pow_four_mul_ltnovelty unverified

theorem pow_four_mul_lt (h : OddPerfect N) {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hpN : p ∣ N) (hqN : q ∣ N) (hpq : p < q) : p ^ 4 * q < 2 * N

Bound for any two prime factors p < q of an odd perfect number: p ^ 4 * q < 2 * N. In particular this holds for the second largest prime Q and the largest prime P: Q ^ 4 * P < 2 * N.

OddPerfect.pow_five_lt_of_lt

theorem pow_five_lt_of_lt (h : OddPerfect N) {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hpN : p ∣ N) (hqN : q ∣ N) (hpq : p < q) : p ^ 5 < 2 * N

Every prime factor p other than the largest one satisfies p ^ 5 < 2 * N; in particular p_{k-1} < (2N)^{1/5}.

OddPerfect.pow_three_lt_three_mul

theorem pow_three_lt_three_mul (h : OddPerfect N) {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hpN : p ∣ N) :
  p ^ 3 < 3 * N

Every prime factor p of an odd perfect number satisfies p ^ 3 < 3 * N. For exponent at least two this follows from p ^ 4 < 2 * N. For p ∥ N, take a component r ^ c with p ∣ σ(r ^ c) = p * s, let r ^ e = gcd(p + 1, r ^ c); then r ^ c * σ(r ^ c) * (p + 1) ≤ 2 N r ^ e, and r ^ e ∣ s + (1 + r + ... + r ^ (e - 1)) forces s large enough.

OddPerfect.pow_four_mul_pow_four_lt

theorem pow_four_mul_pow_four_lt (h : OddPerfect N) {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hpN : p ∣ N) (hqN : q ∣ N) (hpq : p ≠ q) : (p * q) ^ 4 < 6 * N ^ 2

For any two distinct prime factors p ≠ q of an odd perfect number: (p * q) ^ 4 < 6 * N ^ 2. For the two largest primes this is p_{k-1} p_k < 6^{1/4} N^{1/2}.

OddPerfect.CountingClaytonHansen

OddPerfect/CountingClaytonHansen.lean · 1477 lines · 58 public theorems · imports CountingZelinsky, Phi3Algebra

99ω ≤ 37Ω + 187 for every odd perfect number, and 51ω ≤ 19Ω + 46 when 3 does not divide N, after Clayton and Hansen (arXiv 2303.11974).

Proof idea. The primes with exponent exactly 2, other than 3, are sorted by the number of prime factors of p² + p + 1 and by p modulo 3. Each class contributes to, and receives from, the others through the factors of p² + p + 1, and each such relation is one linear inequality, proved as its own lemma. A linking map sends a prime of the small classes to a prime factor of its p² + p + 1; it is injective on one union of classes and at most two to one on a larger one. Three steps of the printed argument needed repair: the lemma linking two of the classes is replaced by two direct lemmas, and the statement that no prime is the largest factor of three values of x² + x + 1 in the required configuration is proved by unique factorization in the Eisenstein integers, carried out by hand on integer pairs. The final ledger of linear relations is closed by integer linear arithmetic.
