The six elements of norm 1.

OddPerfect.eis_unit_coeff

theorem eis_unit_coeff {ε : ℤ × ℤ} {x U V : ℤ} (hε : eisNorm ε = 1)
  (h : (x, -1) = eisMul ε (U, V)) :
  V = 1 ∨ V = -1 ∨ U = 1 ∨ U = -1 ∨ U - V = 1 ∨ U - V = -1

If (x, -1) = ε · (U, V) with ε a unit, then one of V, U, U - V is ±1.

OddPerfect.no_triple_threat

theorem no_triple_threat {x a b c : ℕ} (ha : a.Prime) (hb : b.Prime) (hc : c.Prime)
  (ha2 : a ≠ 2) (hb2 : b ≠ 2) (hc2 : c ≠ 2)
  (ha3 : a % 3 = 2) (hb3 : b % 3 = 2) (hc3 : c % 3 = 2) (hA : (phi3 a).Prime)
  (hB : (phi3 b).Prime) (hC : (phi3 c).Prime) (h : phi3 x = phi3 a * phi3 b * phi3 c) :
  False

No triple threat: for primes a, b, c ≡ 2 (mod 3) with a ^ 2 + a + 1, b ^ 2 + b + 1, c ^ 2 + c + 1 prime, no x satisfies x ^ 2 + x + 1 = (a ^ 2 + a + 1)(b ^ 2 + b + 1)(c ^ 2 + c + 1). Repetitions among a, b, c are allowed. The proof is an explicit descent in ℤ[ω] written on integer pairs.

OddPerfect.ch_uniqueness

theorem ch_uniqueness {a₁ b₁ c₁ a₂ b₂ c₂ d : ℕ} (hc₁ : c₁.Prime) (hc₂ : c₂.Prime)
  (h₁ : phi3 a₁ = c₁ * d) (h₁' : phi3 b₁ = 3 * c₁) (h₂ : phi3 a₂ = c₂ * d)
  (h₂' : phi3 b₂ = 3 * c₂) (hd₁ : d < c₁) (hd₂ : d < c₂) (hd3 : 3 < d) (hb₁ : 3 ≤ b₁)
  (hb₂ : 3 ≤ b₂) : a₁ = a₂

The prime d determines a in a configuration a ^ 2 + a + 1 = c d, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 c, c > d > 3, c prime, b ≥ 3. Indeed b ^ 2 + 7 = 5 b + 3 d and a + 3 = b + d.

OddPerfect.ch_sum_S22_S31

theorem ch_sum_S22_S31 {a c d e g : ℕ} (hd : d.Prime) (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e)
  (hC : phi3 c = d * g) (hde : e ≤ d) (hgd : g < d) (ha : a % 2 = 1) (hc : c % 2 = 1)
  (hac : a ≠ c) : d = a + c + 1

If d is prime, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e with e ≤ d, c ^ 2 + c + 1 = d g with g < d, and a ≠ c are odd, then d = a + c + 1. Two elements of S_{3,1} with largest prime d therefore coincide when an element of S_{2,2} also has largest prime d.

OddPerfect.ch_small_S22_not_S31

theorem ch_small_S22_not_S31 {a b c d f g : ℕ} (hd : d.Prime) (hf : f.Prime)
  (hA : phi3 a = d * f) (hB : phi3 b = 3 * d) (hC : phi3 c = 3 * f * g) (hfd : f < d)
  (hf3 : 3 < f) (hgf : g ≤ f) (ha2 : a % 2 = 1) (hb2 : b % 2 = 1) (hc2 : c % 2 = 1)
  (ha3 : a % 3 = 2) (hc3 : c % 3 = 1) : False

313 ^ 2 + 313 + 1 = 3 · 181 ^ 2). Let d, f be primes with 3 < f < d, a ^ 2 + a + 1 = d f, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 d, with a, b odd and a ≡ 2 (mod 3). Then no odd c ≡ 1 (mod 3) has c ^ 2 + c + 1 = 3 f g with g ≤ f.

OddPerfect.ch_sum_S31_S31

theorem ch_sum_S31_S31 {a b d e f : ℕ} (hd : d.Prime) (hA : phi3 a = 3 * d * e)
  (hB : phi3 b = 3 * d * f) (hde : e ≤ d) (hfd : f ≤ d) (hab : a ≠ b) :
  3 * d = a + b + 1

Two primes a ≠ b with a ^ 2 + a + 1 = 3 d e, b ^ 2 + b + 1 = 3 d f, e, f ≤ d, d prime, satisfy 3 d = a + b + 1.

OddPerfect.bigP_eq

  bigP n = m

bigP n is the largest prime factor of n.

OddPerfect.link_view

theorem link_view {N p : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hp : p ∈ chS1 N ∪ chA N 2 ∪ chB N 2) :

OddPerfect.ch_ledger

theorem ch_ledger {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ s t G f s1 a2 b2 a3 b3 a4 b4 s1s s1t xs xt sone : ℕ,
    N.primeFactors.card = s + t + (if 3 ∣ N then 1 else 0) ∧
    ∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, N.factorization p = 2 * s + G + f ∧
    f = N.factorization 3 ∧ 4 * t ≤ G + 3 ∧
    s = s1 + a2 + b2 + a3 + b3 + a4 + b4 ∧ sone = a2 + a3 + a4 ∧
    s1 ≤ s1s + s1t + 1 ∧ a3 + b3 ≤ xs + xt ∧ sone ≤ f ∧
    s1 + 2 * b2 + 3 * b3 + a4 + 4 * b4 ≤ G + a2 ∧
    s1 + a2 + b2 ≤ sone + t ∧ 2 * (s1 + a2) + b2 + a3 ≤ 2 * (sone + t) ∧
    2 * s1s + xs ≤ 2 * sone ∧ 4 * s1t + xt ≤ G

With s = |S|, t = |T ∪ {p₀}|, G = g₄ + e₀, f = v₃(N), s1 = |S₁|, a2, a3, a4 the sizes of S_{2,1}, S_{3,1}, S_{≥4,1}, b2, b3, b4 those of S_{2,2}, S_{3,2}, S_{≥4,2}, sone = |S_{·,1}|, s1s = |S₁^S|, s1t = |S₁^T|, xs = |X_S|, xt = |X_T|, the listed linear relations hold.

  99 * N.primeFactors.card ≤ 37 * (∑ p ∈ N.primeFactors, N.factorization p) + 187

For an odd perfect number, (99/37) ω - 187/37 ≤ Ω, stated with N.primeFactors.card and the exponent sum.

Exponent patterns

Exclusions of families of exponent patterns for the ordinary primes.

OddPerfect.Seed.ThreeFree

OddPerfect/Seed/ThreeFree.lean · 89 lines · 3 public theorems · imports Seed.Starter

When 3 does not divide a perfect number, exact square primes are ≡ 2 (mod 3), and every prime of σ(p²) is ≡ 1 (mod 3) and not itself an exact square.

Proof idea. If p ≡ 1 (mod 3) then 3 divides 1 + p + p², which would force 3. A prime q ≠ 3 dividing 1 + p + p² gives p³ ≡ 1 with p ≢ 1 modulo q, so p has order 3 and 3 divides q − 1.

Public theorems

OPNThreeFree.source_mod_three

theorem source_mod_three {n p : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ n)
  (hp : p.Prime) (he : n.factorization p = 2) : p % 3 = 2

An exact-square prime in a perfect number coprime to 3 has residue 2 modulo 3.

OPNThreeFree.target_mod_three

theorem target_mod_three {p q : ℕ} (hq : q.Prime) (hq3 : q ≠ 3)
  (hdiv : q ∣ 1 + p + p ^ 2) : q % 3 = 1

A prime divisor other than 3 of the third cyclotomic value has residue 1 mod 3.

OPNThreeFree.exact_square_transfer

theorem exact_square_transfer {n p q : ℕ} (hn : n.Perfect) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ n)
  (hp : p.Prime) (he : n.factorization p = 2) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hdiv : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ 2)) :
  p % 3 = 2 ∧ q ∣ n ∧ q ≠ 3 ∧ q % 3 = 1 ∧ n.factorization q ≠ 2

Every prime forced by an exact square belongs to the support of the perfect number, is 1 modulo 3, and cannot itself be an exact-square component.

OddPerfect.SteuerwaldThree

OddPerfect/SteuerwaldThree.lean · 140 lines · 1 public theorem · imports Euler

The ordinary exponents cannot all equal 2 when 3 divides N.

Proof idea. 3 is ≡ 3 (mod 4), so it is ordinary with exponent 2 and the 3-adic ledger has budget exactly 2. A forcing chain starting at σ(3²) = 13 always produces three distinct non special primes p ≡ 1 (mod 3), each contributing a factor 3 through σ(p²) = 1 + p + p², which overflows the budget. The case split follows the primes 13, 61, 97 of the chain and whether each is special.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.steuerwald_of_three_dvd

theorem steuerwald_of_three_dvd {N q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q))
  (hsq : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p = 2) (h3 : 3 ∣ N) : False

Steuerwald's theorem, case 3 ∣ N.

OddPerfect.Steuerwald

OddPerfect/Steuerwald.lean · 159 lines · 6 public theorems · imports ClosedLoop, SteuerwaldThree, Seed.Example

The ordinary exponents cannot all equal 2; more generally, for an odd prime l not dividing N they cannot all equal l − 1. Hence some prime has an even exponent at least 4.

Proof idea. With l not dividing N and l dividing every ordinary e + 1: no ordinary prime is ≡ 1 (mod l), since such a prime would put a factor l into σ(pl−1). Every prime divisor of σ(pl−1) is l or ≡ 1 (mod l), so it must be the special prime q, and σ(pl−1) is a positive power of q. When all ordinary exponents equal l − 1 this is the situation excluded by ClosedLoop. For l = 3 this is the case where 3 does not divide N; the other case is SteuerwaldThree.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.sigma_pred_prime_eq_special_pow

theorem sigma_pred_prime_eq_special_pow {N q l p : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (_hq : q.Prime)
  (_hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) (hl : l.Prime) (hl2 : l ≠ 2) (hlN : ¬ l ∣ N)
  (hexp : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → l ∣ N.factorization p + 1)
  (hp : p ∈ N.primeFactors) (hpq : p ≠ q) : ∃ c : ℕ, 0 < c ∧ σ 1 (p ^ (l - 1)) = q ^ c

If an odd prime l does not divide N and l ∣ e + 1 for every ordinary exponent e, then every ordinary prime p has σ(p ^ (l - 1)) equal to a positive power of the special prime.

OddPerfect.not_all_exponents_eq_pred_prime

theorem not_all_exponents_eq_pred_prime {N q l : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) (hl : l.Prime) (hl2 : l ≠ 2) (hlN : ¬ l ∣ N)
  (hexp : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p = l - 1) : False

If an odd prime l does not divide N, the ordinary exponents cannot all equal l - 1.

OddPerfect.steuerwald_of_not_three_dvd

theorem steuerwald_of_not_three_dvd {N q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q))
  (hsq : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p = 2) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ N) : False

OddPerfect.steuerwald

theorem steuerwald {N q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q))
  (hsq : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p = 2) : False

Steuerwald: the primes other than the special prime cannot all have exponent two.

Proof idea. If 3 does not divide N, every σ(p²) of an ordinary p is a power of the special prime, which the closed loop argument excludes. If 3 divides N, the 3-adic budget is exactly 2, and a forcing chain from σ(3²) = 13 produces three ordinary primes ≡ 1 (mod 3), each adding a factor 3.

OddPerfect.exists_even_exponent_ge_four

theorem exists_even_exponent_ge_four {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ∃ p ∈ N.primeFactors, Even (N.factorization p) ∧ 4 ≤ N.factorization p

Unconditional form: some prime factor has an even exponent at least four.

In Euler's form N = q^k m^2, the number m is not squarefree.

OddPerfect.McDaniel

OddPerfect/McDaniel.lean · 664 lines · 3 public theorems · imports Euler, Abundancy, Seed.Example, Seed.ThreeFree

The ordinary exponents cannot all be ≡ 2 (mod 3).

Proof idea. Case 3 divides N: each ordinary prime t has exponent at least 2 and forces every odd divisor of t² + t + 1, the special prime q forces every odd divisor of q + 1, and primes ≡ 3 (mod 4) are never special. A finite tree starting from 3 and 13 branches on whether each forced prime (13, 61, 97, 3169, 3348577, 3737657091169) is special, and every leaf exhibits an abundant divisor; large primes carry certified primality witnesses. Case 3 does not divide N: every ordinary p has p² + p + 1 = qc with c ≥ 1, q ≥ 13 and p ≥ 5 give 3c < p, distinct p give distinct c, and the abundancy stays below (13/12)·∏ (1 + 3−c) ≤ (13/12)(8/5) < 2.

  (hexp : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p % 3 = 2) (h3 : 3 ∣ N) : False

OddPerfect.KanoldFour

OddPerfect/KanoldFour.lean · 151 lines · 3 public theorems · imports Certificate, Steuerwald, Valuation, SpecialPrime

The ordinary exponents cannot all equal 4.

Proof idea. If 5 does not divide N, the prime length argument of Steuerwald applies with l = 5. If 5 is ordinary, the 5-adic budget is 4, and the chain σ(5⁴) = 11·71, σ(11⁴) = 5·3221, σ(71⁴) = 5·11·211·2221, σ(211⁴) = 5·1361·292661 yields 11, 71, 211 (all ≡ 3 mod 4, so ordinary) and 1361, 2221, 3221, at most one of which is special; five ordinary primes ≡ 1 (mod 5) each contribute a factor 5, which overflows. If 5 is special with exponent k, then 6 divides σ(5k), so 3 divides N; no σ(p⁴) is divisible by 3, and the 3-adic ledger gives 6 dividing k + 1, so σ(5⁵) = 2·3²·7·31 divides σ(5k), 7 divides N, and 3⁴·5·7⁴ is abundant.

OddPerfect.ExponentSix

OddPerfect/ExponentSix.lean · 206 lines · 4 public theorems · imports Certificate, Steuerwald, SpecialPrime

The ordinary exponents cannot all equal 6.

Proof idea. If 7 does not divide N, the prime length argument applies with l = 7. If 7 divides N, it is ordinary (7 ≡ 3 mod 4) with 7-adic budget 6, and each ordinary prime p ≡ 1 (mod 7) puts a factor 7 into σ(p⁶), so seven such primes overflow. Forcing chains supply them: σ(7⁶) = 29·4733, then 4733 → 70001 → 50359 → 71 → 883 → 379 → 43 when neither 4733 nor 70001 is special. If 4733 is special, 4734 = 2·3²·263 forces 3 and 263, with chains 3 → 1093 → 14939 → 197, 263 → 239, 7211, and 7 → 29 → 88009573. If 70001 is special, 70002 = 2·3²·3889 forces 3, with chains through 1093, 14939, 197, then 29, 88009573 and 4733, 69655517.

OddPerfect.EqualExponents

OddPerfect/EqualExponents.lean · 531 lines · 20 public theorems · imports Steuerwald, Valuation, McDaniel, SpecialPrime

Odd perfect numbers whose ordinary exponents all equal 2β, and general forms: an l-adic counting bound, divisibility by l when every ordinary e + 1 is a power of l, and l⁴ dividing N. The count near the end of the module bounds only the ordinary primes whose σ(p2β) is not a power of the special prime; it does not bound the total number of prime factors.

Proof idea. The counting bound is the l-adic ledger: a prime factor p ≡ 1 (mod l) with exponent e has vl(σ(pe)) = vl(e + 1) by lifting the exponent, and these contributions add up to at most vl(N). Residue avoidance is the forcing step: if p has order l modulo a prime r and l divides e + 1, then r divides σ(pl−1), which divides σ(pe) and hence N.

OddPerfect.orderOf_ne_of_not_dvd

theorem orderOf_ne_of_not_dvd {N p r l : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hp : p.Prime) (hr : r.Prime)
  (hrN : ¬ r ∣ N) (hl : 1 < l) (hle : l ∣ N.factorization p + 1) :
  orderOf (p : ZMod r) ≠ l

Residue avoidance. If a prime r does not divide the odd perfect number N, 1 < l and l ∣ v_p(N) + 1, then p does not have multiplicative order l modulo r. (Order l would force r ∣ σ(p ^ v_p(N)) ∣ 2 N.)

OddPerfect.card_not_special_pow_le

theorem card_not_special_pow_le {N q l β : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hl : l.Prime)
  (hβ : 2 * β + 1 = l) (hexp : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → N.factorization p = 2 * β)
  (hlN : l ∈ N.primeFactors) (hlq : l ≠ q) :

Components that are not powers of the special prime. OddPerfect.ThreeFreeException

OddPerfect/ThreeFreeException.lean · 617 lines · 1 public theorem · imports Abundancy, Euler, Seed.ThreeFree, Seed.Example contains novelty unverified

If every prime other than q and one further prime r has exponent exactly 2, then 3 divides N.

Proof idea. Assume 3 does not divide N. Then q ≡ 1 (mod 12), so q ≥ 13, and for every other ordinary p, σ(p²) = qarb with a, b not both even, since 1 + p + p² lies strictly between consecutive squares. If r divides no σ(p²), the σ(p²) are odd powers of q, consecutive ordinary primes grow by a factor at least 13, and the abundancy is below (13/12)(5/4)(7/5) = 91/48 < 2. Otherwise r ≡ 1 (mod 3), the map p ↦ (a, b) is injective with (p + 1)² > 13arb, and a box count for small (a, b) plus a geometric tail bound the abundancy below 2. The argument uses only elementary size bounds.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.three_dvd_of_one_exceptionnovelty unverified

theorem three_dvd_of_one_exception {N q r : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) (hr : r.Prime) (hrq : r ≠ q)
  (hsq : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → p ≠ r → N.factorization p = 2) : 3 ∣ N

A restrained odd perfect number is divisible by 3.

Proof idea. Assume 3 does not divide N. Then q ≡ 1 (mod 12), and for every other ordinary p, σ(p²) = qarb. Size comparisons on these values, split by whether r divides some σ(p²), bound the abundancy below 2. No computer search is used.

OddPerfect.ThreeFreeCert

OddPerfect/ThreeFreeCert.lean · 159 lines · 8 public theorems · imports Certificate

Certificates excluding exponent 2 at 5, 11, 29 and a fourth prime x: outright for x ∈ {17, 23, 47}, and for x ∈ {41, 53} when 3 does not divide N.

Proof idea. Each certificate forces primes from the exact squares (σ(5²) = 31, σ(11²) = 7·19, σ(29²) = 13·67, and for example σ(17²) = 307 or σ(23²) = 7·79) and closes with an abundant divisor that takes every forced prime to the first power. The certificates were produced by an untrusted program and are accepted by check through decide +kernel.

OddPerfect.AttackSize

OddPerfect/AttackSize.lean · 542 lines · 6 public theorems · imports Abundancy, Euler, Network, ExponentCap, Steuerwald, Seed.ThreeFree contains novelty unverified

Results that mix exact arithmetic with size. When 3 does not divide N, primes p with p² exactly dividing N and σ(p²) a power of the special prime carry little abundancy, so 3 divides N if every ordinary exponent is 2 except for at most twelve primes ≡ 2 (mod 3), or except for at most four primes that divide no σ(p²) of the exponent two primes. For every odd perfect number, a proper set S of prime factors that are all at least p gives 2p2|S| < 3N, and if every prime factor is at least p then p2ω(N)+1 ≤ N.

Proof idea. For the first part, call p a pure square when p² exactly divides N and 1 + p + p² is a power qa of the special prime. Then q ≡ 1 (mod 12), and p ≡ 2 (mod 3) and p ≡ 3 (mod 4), so p ≡ 11 (mod 12); 11, 23 and 47 fail because 133 = 7·19, 553 = 7·79 and 2257 = 37·61, so p ≥ 59. The exponent a is odd since 1 + p + p² lies strictly between two squares, so two pure squares p < p' satisfy 13p ≤ p'. The pure squares therefore contribute at most 61/59 to the abundancy, and the remaining primes must have ∏ p/(p − 1) > 1416/793. For the second part, the components over S multiply to at least p2|S|−1, because at most one exponent is odd. Strong connectivity of the network gives an edge from some component lb outside S into S, so σ(lb) ≥ p, while σ(lb) < (3/2)lb. Multiplying gives 2p2|S| < 3N.

theorem prod_nonpure_gt_of_not_three_dvd {N q : ℕ} {Y : Finset ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N)
  (hq : q.Prime) (hodd : Odd (N.factorization q)) (h3 : ¬ 3 ∣ N)
  (hY : ∀ p ∈ N.primeFactors, p ≠ q → p ∉ Y →
    N.factorization p = 2 ∧ ∃ a, σ 1 (p ^ 2) = q ^ a) :
  (1416 / 793 : ℚ) < ∏ p ∈ (N.primeFactors.erase q).filter (· ∈ Y), (p : ℚ) / (p - 1)

Pure squares carry little abundancy. Let N be an odd perfect number with special prime q and 3 ∤ N, and let Y be any set containing every prime factor p ≠ q that is not a pure square (a pure square has p ^ 2 ∥ N and σ(p ^ 2) a power of q). Then the prime factors of N in Y other than q satisfy ∏ p/(p-1) > 1416/793 ≈ 1.7856.

OddPerfect.ExponentTenTwelve

OddPerfect/ExponentTenTwelve.lean · 659 lines · 4 public theorems · imports Certificate, Steuerwald, Valuation, SpecialPrime, Pratt contains novelty unverified

The ordinary exponents cannot all equal 10 and cannot all equal 12. Also, for every prime l, the special prime cannot be l when every ordinary exponent equals l − 1.

Proof idea. Write l for 11 or 13. If l does not divide N, the prime length argument of Steuerwald applies. If l is the special prime, write N = lkRl − 1; every prime factor of the product of the ordinary component divisor sums is l or ≡ 1 (mod l), and removing the powers of l from σ(N) = 2N leaves an identity that reads 1 = 2 modulo l. If l is ordinary, its budget is l − 1, and every ordinary prime ≡ 1 (mod l) spends one unit, so at most l such primes divide N. Forcing chains then exhibit l + 1 of them, starting from σ(11¹⁰) = 15797·1806113 and σ(13¹²) = 53·264031·1803647, after a case split on which forced prime ≡ 1 (mod 4) is special. Large primes carry certificates checked by Pratt.

OddPerfect.UniformExponents

OddPerfect/UniformExponents.lean · 812 lines · 21 public theorems · imports EqualExponents, SmallestPrime contains novelty unverified

Structure of a hypothetical odd perfect number whose ordinary exponents all equal l − 1, for every odd prime l at once. The ordinary primes split into S (those ≡ 1 mod l) and W (the others, apart from l). Results: every prime of W receives its whole budget from the special component, so 2(∏W)l − 1 divides σ(qk); the size of S is bounded; W is nonempty, with at least two elements for l ≥ 53; and the exact l-adic ledger |S| + vl(k + 1) = l − 1 holds when l is ordinary.

Proof idea. No ordinary component divisor sum is divisible by a prime w of W, because an ordinary σ(pl − 1) only has prime factors equal to l or ≡ 1 (mod l). So the w-adic ledger has a single term, vw(σ(qk)) = l − 1. Each prime of S spends one unit of the budget of l, which bounds |S| and hence the product of s/(s − 1) over S. Abundancy 2 must then be supplied by q, l and the primes of W, which forces W to be nonempty and gives the lower bounds on |W|. Lifting the exponent turns vw(σ(qk)) = l − 1 into exact statements about vw(k + 1) and the order of q modulo w.

theorem card_congrPrimes_add_padicValNat {N q l : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hq : q.Prime)

OddPerfect.KanoldGeneral

OddPerfect/KanoldGeneral.lean · 503 lines · 10 public theorems · imports EqualExponents, Zsigmondy, Abundancy contains novelty unverified

If t divides e + 1 for every ordinary exponent e, then t⁴ divides N. Also: if an odd prime l divides every e + 1 then l divides N, the prime power form l4a | N, and two cases of the stronger fifth power statement.

Proof idea. Suppose an odd prime l divides every ordinary e + 1 but not N. Then every Φl(p) = 1 + p + … + pl − 1 with p ordinary is a power of the special prime q. A growth lemma shows that such primes are sparse: for ordinary p < p′, both are l-th roots of unity modulo M = Φl(p), the unit group modulo a power of q is cyclic, so p′ is congruent to a power pj with j < l, and p′ < M would force p′ = pj. Hence Φl(p) < p′, the ordinary primes grow faster than squaring, and the abundancy stays below 2. For the fourth power: if l is ordinary its exponent is at least la − 1; if l = q, primitive prime factors of the cyclotomic values at q, q², …, qa of one ordinary prime give a + 1 primes ≡ 1 (mod q), each of which spends a units, and the residue of the special exponent modulo 4 finishes.

OddPerfect.lt_of_geom_eq_pownovelty unverified

theorem lt_of_geom_eq_pow {l q p p' c c' : ℕ} (hl : l.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hq2 : q ≠ 2)
  (hp : p.Prime) (hp' : p'.Prime) (hpp' : p < p') (hp'q : p' ≠ q)
  (hc : ∑ i ∈ Finset.range l, p ^ i = q ^ c) (hc' : ∑ i ∈ Finset.range l, p' ^ i = q ^ c') :
  ∑ i ∈ Finset.range l, p ^ i < p'

Growth of primes whose Φ_l values are powers of one odd prime. Let l be a prime, q an odd prime, and p < p' primes with p' ≠ q such that 1 + p + ... + p ^ (l - 1) and 1 + p' + ... + p' ^ (l - 1) are both powers of q. Then 1 + p + ... + p ^ (l - 1) < p'.

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed

OddPerfect/ForcingClosed.lean · 527 lines · 15 public theorems · imports Euler, Abundancy, Zsigmondy, Pratt contains novelty unverified

A relaxation of the problem. A forcing closed configuration is a finite set of odd primes with exponents that satisfies four conditions: Euler's parity pattern, closure (every odd prime factor of a component divisor sum is in the set), and total abundancy at most 2. The exact valuation ledger is dropped. Every odd perfect number gives a configuration, so nonexistence of configurations (the statement C1, a definition that is never assumed and may be false) would imply the conjecture. Proved for configurations: every exponent is below the largest prime of the set, the exponents can be reduced to 1 for the special prime and l − 1 with l prime for the others, and the ordinary exponents e cannot all satisfy 3 | e + 1.

Proof idea. Closure and a primitive prime factor give, for each exponent e ≥ 2, a prime r ≡ 1 (mod e + 1) in the set, so e + 2 ≤ r. Reduction uses that σ(pa) divides σ(pb) when a + 1 divides b + 1, so closure and the size bound survive when exponents shrink. If every ordinary e has 3 | e + 1, then p² + p + 1 divides every ordinary component divisor sum; closure and parity force 3 into the set, and a finite forcing tree starting at 3 produces a subset whose abundancy already exceeds 2. No valuation count is used, only closure, parity and size.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.forcingClosed_of_oddPerfect

theorem forcingClosed_of_oddPerfect {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) :
  ForcingClosed N.primeFactors N.factorization ∧
    ∏ s ∈ N.primeFactors, abundancy (s ^ N.factorization s) = 2

An odd perfect number gives a forcing closed configuration (its prime factors with their exact exponents), and the product of the component abundancies is exactly two.

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed.of_dvd

theorem of_dvd (hF : ForcingClosed S e) {e' : ℕ → ℕ} (q : ℕ) (hq : q ∈ S) (hq4 : q % 4 = 1)
  (he'q : e' q % 4 = 1) (hev : ∀ s ∈ S, s ≠ q → e' s % 2 = 0)
  (hpos : ∀ s ∈ S, 0 < e' s) (hdvd : ∀ s ∈ S, e' s + 1 ∣ e s + 1) :
  ForcingClosed S e'

Shrinking exponents along row divisibility preserves forcing closure: if every new exponent e' s is positive, keeps the parity pattern, and has e' s + 1 ∣ e s + 1, the new configuration is again forcing closed.

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed.reducenovelty unverified

theorem reduce (hF : ForcingClosed S e) :
  ∃ q ∈ S, q % 4 = 1 ∧ e q % 4 = 1 ∧ (∀ s ∈ S, s ≠ q → e s % 2 = 0) ∧
    ForcingClosed S (reduceExp q e) ∧ reduceExp q e q = 1 ∧
    ∀ s ∈ S, s ≠ q → (reduceExp q e s + 1).Prime ∧ reduceExp q e s + 1 ≠ 2

Reduction: every forcing closed configuration has a reduced one on the same support, with special exponent 1 and every ordinary exponent of the form l - 1, l an odd prime. It also returns the special node q of the original configuration with its parity data. So C1 is equivalent to C1 for reduced configurations.

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed.three_mem

theorem three_mem (hF : ForcingClosed S e) (h3 : ∀ s ∈ S, e s % 2 = 0 → 3 ∣ e s + 1) :
  3 ∈ S

Closure and parity alone (no size): if every ordinary exponent e s has 3 ∣ e s + 1 (for example all ordinary exponents equal 2), then 3 is a node. Proof: otherwise every ordinary node is ≢ 1 (mod 3), so every prime factor of s ^ 2 + s + 1 is the special node q; an odd prime s ∣ q + 1 then gives s ^ 2 + s + 1 = q, so s ∣ 2.

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed.false_of_sq

OddPerfect.ForcingClosed.exists_not_three_dvd_succnovelty unverified

theorem exists_not_three_dvd_succ (hF : ForcingClosed S e) :
  ∃ s ∈ S, e s % 2 = 0 ∧ ¬ 3 ∣ e s + 1

McDaniel and Steuerwald for forcing closed configurations. In every forcing closed configuration some ordinary exponent e s (even) has 3 ∤ e s + 1. In particular the ordinary exponents cannot all equal 2 (Steuerwald) and cannot all be ≡ 2 (mod 3) (McDaniel). The proof uses only closure, parity and size: no valuation ledger.

Consequence for odd perfect numbers, through forcing closed configurations: some ordinary exponent e has 3 ∤ e + 1. This recovers Steuerwald and McDaniel.

Collisions of x² + x + 1

Partial results on questions from Ochem and Zelinsky (arXiv 2607.19746) about values of x² + x + 1, and numerical checks.

OddPerfect.Seed.Collision

OddPerfect/Seed/Collision.lean · 72 lines · 2 public theorems

Square ratios of values of x² + x + 1: if x² + x + 1 = k²(y² + y + 1) with k > 1 then y < k, and an even exponent gap forces a > 2b.

Proof idea. With u = 2x + 1 and v = k(2y + 1), the relation becomes u² + 3 = v² + 3k², so v < u, and comparing with (v + 1)² bounds y below k. For values par and pbr with a = b + 2t, the ratio is (pt)², so y < pt and y² + y + 1 < p2t, which is impossible if 2t ≤ b.

  (h : phi3 x = k ^ 2 * phi3 y) : y < k

A nontrivial square ratio forces the smaller input below the square root of the ratio.

OPNCollision.even_gap_forces_exponent_bound

theorem even_gap_forces_exponent_bound {x y p r a b t : ℕ}
  (hp : 1 < p) (hr : 0 < r) (ht : 0 < t) (hgap : a = b + 2 * t)
  (hx : phi3 x = p ^ a * r) (hy : phi3 y = p ^ b * r) :
  2 * b < a

Two values with a common positive cofactor and a positive even exponent gap must have the larger exponent strictly greater than twice the smaller.

OddPerfect.Seed.Gap

OddPerfect/Seed/Gap.lean · 139 lines · 3 public theorems

An odd prime power dividing y² + y + 1 (y ≥ 3) cannot be the square ratio to another value, so an even gap 2k needs k > b.

Proof idea. Write u = 2x + 1 = pv + t with v = 2y + 1 and p the prime power. Then t is even and 2pvt + t² + 3 = 3p², which forces 4t < p and t < v, so 0 < v − t and v + t < p. But p divides both v² + 3 and t² + 3, hence (v − t)(v + t), and for a prime power coprime to v it must divide one factor, a contradiction.

  x^2 + x + 1 ≠ (q^k)^2 * (y^2 + y + 1)

If an odd prime power q^k > 1 divides the polynomial value at y ≥ 3, multiplication by its square cannot give another value of the polynomial.

OPNGap.no_even_gap_of_le

theorem no_even_gap_of_le {x y q r b k : ℕ} (hy : 3 ≤ y)

The exponent-gap formulation. For a positive even gap a-b = 2*k, any solution in the stated domain must have k > b, hence a > 3*b.

OddPerfect.CollisionGap

OddPerfect/CollisionGap.lean · 332 lines · 4 public theorems · imports Seed.Collision contains novelty unverified

Sharper bound in the even gap case: a common prime power of K and y² + y + 1 forces K > pb(y² + y + 1), so k > 2b and a > 5b. Full impossibility is not proved.

Proof idea. For x² + x + 1 = K²(y² + y + 1) with K ≥ 2, write V = 2y + 1 and 2x + 1 = KV + t; then t² + 3 = Km and 2Vt + m = 3K for an odd m. A prime power pb dividing K and y² + y + 1 gives t ≡ ±V and so m ≡ ±6 modulo pb, hence pb ≤ m + 6, and the identity (3K − m)² = 4V²(Km − 3) then forces K > pb(y² + y + 1) when y ≥ 3. The bound y ≥ 3 cannot be dropped: 18² + 18 + 1 = 7²(2² + 2 + 1).

Public theorems

OddPerfect.phi3_square_ratio_param

theorem phi3_square_ratio_param {x y K : ℕ} (hK : 2 ≤ K)
  (h : x ^ 2 + x + 1 = K ^ 2 * (y ^ 2 + y + 1)) :
  ∃ t m : ℕ, 0 < t ∧ t % 2 = 0 ∧ m % 2 = 1 ∧ 2 * x + 1 = K * (2 * y + 1) + t ∧
    t ^ 2 + 3 = K * m ∧ 2 * (2 * y + 1) * t + m = 3 * K

Parametrization of a square ratio. If x^2+x+1 = K^2 (y^2+y+1) with K ≥ 2, then there are natural numbers t > 0 (even) and m (odd) with 2x+1 = K(2y+1) + t, t^2 + 3 = K m and 2(2y+1) t + m = 3K. No primality is assumed.

OddPerfect.common_prime_power_forces_large_scale

theorem common_prime_power_forces_large_scale {x y K p b : ℕ} (hy : 3 ≤ y) (hp : p.Prime)
  (hb : 0 < b) (hqK : p ^ b ∣ K) (hqF : p ^ b ∣ y ^ 2 + y + 1)
  (h : x ^ 2 + x + 1 = K ^ 2 * (y ^ 2 + y + 1)) :
  p ^ b * (y ^ 2 + y + 1) < K

A common prime power forces a large scale. Suppose x^2+x+1 = K^2 (y^2+y+1) with y ≥ 3, and suppose a prime power p^b with b ≥ 1 divides both K and y^2+y+1. Then p^b (y^2+y+1) < K. No hypothesis on x or on the other factors of K is needed. The bound y ≥ 3 cannot be dropped: 18^2+18+1 = 7^2 (2^2+2+1).

OddPerfect.even_gap_scale_bound

theorem even_gap_scale_bound {x y p r b k : ℕ} (hy : 3 ≤ y) (hp : p.Prime) (hb : 0 < b)
  (hk : 0 < k) (hx : x ^ 2 + x + 1 = p ^ (b + 2 * k) * r)
  (hyv : y ^ 2 + y + 1 = p ^ b * r) : p ^ (2 * b) * r < p ^ k

Suppose p is prime, b, k ≥ 1, y ≥ 3, x^2+x+1 = p^(b+2k) r and y^2+y+1 = p^b r. Then p^(2b) r < p^k. Neither x, y nor r needs to be prime, and r is any natural number.

OddPerfect.even_gap_exponent_boundnovelty unverified

theorem even_gap_exponent_bound {x y p r b k : ℕ} (hy : 3 ≤ y) (hp : p.Prime) (hb : 0 < b)
  (hk : 0 < k) (hx : x ^ 2 + x + 1 = p ^ (b + 2 * k) * r)
  (hyv : y ^ 2 + y + 1 = p ^ b * r) : 2 * b < k ∧ 5 * b < b + 2 * k

Exponent form of even_gap_scale_bound: under the same hypotheses 2b < k, so the larger exponent a = b + 2k satisfies 5b < a.

OddPerfect.Seed.Numeric

OddPerfect/Seed/Numeric.lean · 81 lines · 5 public theorems

Numerical checks for a section of Ochem and Zelinsky: a tail bound for abundancy and an exact factor identity.

Proof idea. (1 − mx)(1 + x)m ≤ 1 by induction on m gives a rational bound for each block of a tail product, and one cross multiplied integer inequality over 100 blocks is decided by computation. In restrained_tail the bound A ≤ 1.089 on the ordinary prime product is a hypothesis: the sieve that would supply it is not reconstructed in Lean.

  (1 - (m : ℚ) * x) * (1 + x)^m ≤ 1

For x ≥ 0 one has (1−mx)(1+x)^m ≤ 1, by induction on m.

  3 * 6000001 * 1089 * (216171986291 : ℕ)^100 <
  4 * 6000000 * 1000 * (216171986291 - 435000000 : ℕ)^100

Cross-multiplied check for 100 blocks, with z = 216171986291 and block length 435000000.

OPNNumeric.restrained_tail

theorem restrained_tail {A : ℚ} (hA : A ≤ 1089 / 1000) :
  (3/2 : ℚ) * (1+1/6000000) * A * (1+1/216171986291)^43500000000 < 2

For every rational A with A ≤ 1.089, the product (3/2) · (1 + 1/6000000) · A · (1 + 1/216171986291)^43500000000 is strictly smaller than two. The bound A ≤ 1.089 is a hypothesis of this theorem, not a result of this file. The file does not reconstruct the sieve of Ochem and Zelinsky that is meant to produce such a bound, does not prove that the sieve's survivor list is exhaustive, and does not identify any computed product with A. The theorem is only the final arithmetic step, valid for whatever A satisfies the hypothesis.

OddPerfect.VietaPairs

OddPerfect/VietaPairs.lean · 620 lines · 33 public theorems · imports Phi3

Pairs p, q with p | q² + q + 1 and q | p² + p + 1. With p ≤ q they are exactly the consecutive terms of the sequence 1, 1, 3, 13, 61, 291, 1393, … defined by t₀ = t₁ = 1 and tn+2 = 5tn+1 − tn − 1, and every such pair satisfies p² + q² + p + q + 1 = 5pq and is coprime. Also residues of the sequence modulo 3, 4 and 5, its growth, and consequences for primes with exponent 2 that feed each other in an odd perfect number.

Proof idea. Consecutive terms satisfy the invariant p² + q² + p + q + 1 = 5pq by induction, and the invariant gives both divisibilities directly. For the converse, a pair with p < q is replaced by the smaller pair ((p² + p + 1)/q, p), which again satisfies both divisibilities; the descent stops when the two entries are equal, which forces (1, 1), and running it backwards places the pair in the sequence.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_mul

theorem quasiSeq_mul (n : ℕ) : quasiSeq n * quasiSeq (n + 2) = phi3 (quasiSeq (n + 1))

The product form of the recursion: t n * t (n + 2) = phi3 (t (n + 1)).

OddPerfect.dvd_phi3_of_invariant

theorem dvd_phi3_of_invariant {p q : ℕ} (h : p ^ 2 + q ^ 2 + p + q + 1 = 5 * p * q) :
  p ∣ phi3 q ∧ q ∣ phi3 p

The equation p² + q² + p + q + 1 = 5 p q implies the two divisibilities.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_dvd

theorem quasiSeq_dvd (n : ℕ) :
  quasiSeq n ∣ phi3 (quasiSeq (n + 1)) ∧ quasiSeq (n + 1) ∣ phi3 (quasiSeq n)

Consecutive terms form a quasisolution.

OddPerfect.exists_quasiSeq_of_dvd

theorem exists_quasiSeq_of_dvd {p q : ℕ} (hpq : p ≤ q) (h1 : p ∣ phi3 q)
  (h2 : q ∣ phi3 p) : ∃ n, p = quasiSeq n ∧ q = quasiSeq (n + 1)

Classification of quasisolutions: if p ≤ q, p ∣ phi3 q and q ∣ phi3 p then (p, q) is a consecutive pair of quasiSeq.

OddPerfect.dvd_phi3_iff_quasiSeq

theorem dvd_phi3_iff_quasiSeq {p q : ℕ} (hpq : p ≤ q) :
  (p ∣ phi3 q ∧ q ∣ phi3 p) ↔ ∃ n, p = quasiSeq n ∧ q = quasiSeq (n + 1)

Quasisolutions with p ≤ q are exactly consecutive pairs of quasiSeq.

OddPerfect.invariant_of_dvd_phi3

theorem invariant_of_dvd_phi3 {p q : ℕ} (h1 : p ∣ phi3 q) (h2 : q ∣ phi3 p) :
  p ^ 2 + q ^ 2 + p + q + 1 = 5 * p * q

Every quasisolution satisfies p² + q² + p + q + 1 = 5 p q. No positivity or other side condition is needed.

OddPerfect.dvd_phi3_iff_invariant

theorem dvd_phi3_iff_invariant {p q : ℕ} :
  (p ∣ phi3 q ∧ q ∣ phi3 p) ↔ p ^ 2 + q ^ 2 + p + q + 1 = 5 * p * q

A pair with p ∣ phi3 q and q ∣ phi3 p is characterized by the equation p² + q² + p + q + 1 = 5 p q.

OddPerfect.coprime_of_dvd_phi3

theorem coprime_of_dvd_phi3 {p q : ℕ} (h1 : p ∣ phi3 q) : Nat.Coprime p q

The two entries of a quasisolution are coprime.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_mod_three

theorem quasiSeq_mod_three (n : ℕ) :
  quasiSeq n % 3 = if n % 3 = 2 then 0 else 1

quasiSeq n is divisible by 3 when n ≡ 2 (mod 3) and is 1 (mod 3) otherwise.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_mod_four

theorem quasiSeq_mod_four (n : ℕ) :
  quasiSeq n % 4 = if n % 3 = 2 then 3 else 1

quasiSeq n is 3 (mod 4) when n ≡ 2 (mod 3) and is 1 (mod 4) otherwise.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_mod_five

theorem quasiSeq_mod_five (n : ℕ) :
  quasiSeq n % 5 = if n % 4 < 2 then 1 else 3

quasiSeq n is 1 (mod 5) when n ≡ 0, 1 (mod 4) and 3 (mod 5) otherwise.

OddPerfect.three_dvd_quasiSeq_iff

theorem three_dvd_quasiSeq_iff (n : ℕ) : 3 ∣ quasiSeq n ↔ n % 3 = 2

3 ∣ quasiSeq n exactly when n ≡ 2 (mod 3).

OddPerfect.growth_of_invariant

theorem growth_of_invariant {p q : ℕ} (hp : 2 ≤ p) (hpq : p ≤ q)
  (h : p ^ 2 + q ^ 2 + p + q + 1 = 5 * p * q) : 4 * p < q ∧ q < 5 * p

If 2 ≤ p ≤ q then 4 p < q < 5 p.

OddPerfect.three_le_quasiSeq

theorem three_le_quasiSeq {n : ℕ} (hn : 2 ≤ n) : 3 ≤ quasiSeq n

From index 2 on every term is at least 3.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_growth

theorem quasiSeq_growth {n : ℕ} (hn : 2 ≤ n) :
  4 * quasiSeq n < quasiSeq (n + 1) ∧ quasiSeq (n + 1) < 5 * quasiSeq n

Growth: 4 t n < t (n + 1) < 5 t n for n ≥ 2. This fails for n = 0, 1.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_lt_succ

theorem quasiSeq_lt_succ {n : ℕ} (hn : 1 ≤ n) : quasiSeq n < quasiSeq (n + 1)

The sequence is strictly increasing from index 1.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_inj

theorem quasiSeq_inj {a b : ℕ} (ha : 1 ≤ a) (hb : 1 ≤ b) (h : quasiSeq a = quasiSeq b) :
  a = b

The sequence is injective on indices at least 1.

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_prime_pair_index

theorem quasiSeq_prime_pair_index {n : ℕ} (h1 : (quasiSeq n).Prime)
  (h2 : (quasiSeq (n + 1)).Prime) : n = 2 ∨ n % 3 = 0

If two consecutive terms are both prime, then n = 2 or n ≡ 0 (mod 3).

OddPerfect.quasiSeq_prime_pair_iff

theorem quasiSeq_prime_pair_iff {n : ℕ} (hn : n ≤ 20) :
  ((quasiSeq n).Prime ∧ (quasiSeq (n + 1)).Prime) ↔ n = 2 ∨ n = 3

The prime pairs among the first terms: for n ≤ 20, both quasiSeq n and quasiSeq (n + 1) are prime exactly for n = 2 (the pair (3, 13)) and n = 3 (the pair (13, 61)). The paper reports a further pair at n = 21; that is not verified here.

OddPerfect.not_sq_dvd_phi3_pair

theorem not_sq_dvd_phi3_pair {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (h1 : p ^ 2 ∣ phi3 q) (h2 : q ∣ phi3 p) : False

There are no primes p, q with p ^ 2 ∣ phi3 q and q ∣ phi3 p.

OddPerfect.chained_prime_pairs

theorem chained_prime_pairs {p q r : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hr : r.Prime)
  (hpr : p ≠ r) (h1 : p ∣ phi3 q) (h2 : q ∣ phi3 p) (h3 : r ∣ phi3 q) (h4 : q ∣ phi3 r) :
  q = 13 ∧ ((p = 3 ∧ r = 61) ∨ (p = 61 ∧ r = 3))

If (p, q) and (q, r) are both mutual divisibility pairs of primes with p ≠ r, then q = 13 and {p, r} = {3, 61}.

OddPerfect.prime_of_dvd_phi3_pair_mod_four

theorem prime_of_dvd_phi3_pair_mod_four {p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (h1 : p ∣ phi3 q)
  (h2 : q ∣ phi3 p) : p = 3 ∨ p % 4 = 1

A prime in a mutual divisibility pair is 3 or is 1 (mod 4).

OddPerfect.exists_quasiSeq_of_sq_pair

theorem exists_quasiSeq_of_sq_pair {N p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hpq : p ≤ q)
  (hep : N.factorization p = 2) (heq : N.factorization q = 2)
  (h1 : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) (h2 : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q)) :
  ∃ n, p = quasiSeq n ∧ q = quasiSeq (n + 1)

Two exact-square prime factors of N that feed each other (q ∣ σ(p²) and p ∣ σ(q²)), with p ≤ q, form a consecutive pair of quasiSeq.

OddPerfect.three_dvd_of_sq_pair

theorem three_dvd_of_sq_pair {N p q : ℕ} (hN : N.Perfect) (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hep : N.factorization p = 2) (heq : N.factorization q = 2)
  (h2 : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q)) : 3 ∣ N

If two exact-square prime factors of a perfect number feed each other, then 3 ∣ N. (This uses only p ∣ σ(q²), not the classification.)

OddPerfect.sigmaWeight_eq_one_of_sq_pair

theorem sigmaWeight_eq_one_of_sq_pair {N p q : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime)
  (hep : N.factorization p = 2) (heq : N.factorization q = 2)
  (h1 : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) (h2 : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q)) :
  sigmaWeight N p q = 1

If two exact-square prime factors p, q of N feed each other, the edge p → q has weight exactly one: q ^ 2 ∤ σ(p²).

OddPerfect.two_le_factorization_three_of_sq_pair

theorem two_le_factorization_three_of_sq_pair {N p q : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N) (hp : p.Prime)
  (hq : q.Prime) (hep : N.factorization p = 2) (heq : N.factorization q = 2)
  (h1 : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) (h2 : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q)) :
  2 ≤ N.factorization 3

If two exact-square prime factors of an odd perfect number feed each other, then 9 ∣ N, i.e. 2 ≤ v₃(N): either one of them is 3 (with exponent 2), or both are 1 (mod 3) and each sends one unit of weight to 3.

OddPerfect.sq_pair_three

theorem sq_pair_three {N q : ℕ} (hN : N.Perfect) (hq : q.Prime)
  (heq : N.factorization q = 2) (h1 : q ∣ σ 1 (3 ^ N.factorization 3))
  (he3 : N.factorization 3 = 2) : q = 13 ∧ 61 ∣ N

If 3 and a prime q are exact-square prime factors of a perfect number with q ∣ σ(3²) (in particular if they feed each other), then q = 13 and 61 ∣ N.

OddPerfect.chained_sq_pairs

theorem chained_sq_pairs {N p q r : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (hq : q.Prime) (hr : r.Prime)
  (hpr : p ≠ r) (hep : N.factorization p = 2) (heq : N.factorization q = 2)
  (her : N.factorization r = 2)
  (h1 : q ∣ σ 1 (p ^ N.factorization p)) (h2 : p ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q))
  (h3 : q ∣ σ 1 (r ^ N.factorization r)) (h4 : r ∣ σ 1 (q ^ N.factorization q)) :
  q = 13 ∧ ((p = 3 ∧ r = 61) ∨ (p = 61 ∧ r = 3))

If p, q, r are distinct exact-square prime factors with p, q feeding each other and q, r feeding each other, then q = 13 and {p, r} = {3, 61}.

Tools and countermodels

Verified checkers, primality certificates, and spoof factorizations kept as test cases.

OddPerfect.Certificate

OddPerfect/Certificate.lean · 364 lines · 12 public theorems · imports Euler, Seed.Example tool

A verified checker for factor chain certificates about an unknown odd perfect number: two step rules, six closing rules, and a soundness theorem. An accepted certificate excludes only the odd perfect numbers that satisfy its root facts; no family of root facts is proved to cover every odd perfect number.

Proof idea. check is a Boolean function evaluated by the kernel; check_sound proves by induction on the certificate tree that acceptance for a state rules out every odd perfect number satisfying the facts of the state. Each rule is justified on the Methods page.

Public theorems

OddPerfect.Cert.isPrimeB_sound

theorem isPrimeB_sound {n : ℕ} (h : isPrimeB n = true) : n.Prime

The trial division test is sound.

OddPerfect.Cert.geom_eq

theorem geom_eq (p e : ℕ) : geom p e = ∑ i ∈ Finset.range (e + 1), p ^ i

geom is the geometric sum.

OddPerfect.Cert.sigma_eq_geom

theorem sigma_eq_geom {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (e : ℕ) : σ 1 (p ^ e) = geom p e

For prime p, σ (p ^ e) = geom p e.

  (h : s.contains f = true) : f.Holds N

A fact found in a valid state holds.

  (h : hasPow s p e = true) : p ^ e ∣ N

  (h : (l.all fun x => x.1 != p) = true) : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1 ≠ p

Unpacks the distinctness test used by the checker.

OddPerfect.Cert.coprime_dprod

theorem coprime_dprod {s : List Fact} {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) :
  ∀ {l : List (ℕ × ℕ)}, abundantOK s l = true → (∀ x ∈ l, x.1 ≠ p) →
    Nat.Coprime p (dprod l)

A prime outside the list is coprime to its recorded divisor.

OddPerfect.Cert.dprod_sound

theorem dprod_sound {s : List Fact} {N : ℕ} (hs : State.Holds s N) :
  ∀ {l : List (ℕ × ℕ)}, abundantOK s l = true → dprod l ∣ N ∧ σ 1 (dprod l) = sprod l

The recorded divisor divides N, and its divisor sum is sprod.

OddPerfect.Cert.overflow_sum

theorem overflow_sum {s : List Fact} {N q : ℕ} (hs : State.Holds s N) (hN : N ≠ 0)
  (hq : q.Prime) :
  ∀ {l : List (ℕ × ℕ × ℕ)}, overflowOK s q l = true →
    (l.map (·.1)).toFinset ⊆ N.primeFactors ∧
      ksum l ≤ ∑ p ∈ (l.map (·.1)).toFinset, incoming N q p

The listed primes divide N, and the claimed valuations are bounded by the true q-adic contributions of their components.

OddPerfect.Cert.check_sound

theorem check_sound {s : List Fact} {c : Cert} (hc : check s c = true) :
  ∀ N : ℕ, OddPerfect N → State.Holds s N → False

Soundness: a certificate accepted for the state s excludes every odd perfect number satisfying all facts of s. That is its whole scope: it says nothing about odd perfect numbers that fail some fact of s. This file proves no family of root states exhaustive over all odd perfect numbers; coverage has to come from a separate theorem.

Proof idea. Induction on the certificate tree. A step rule adds a fact that holds for every odd perfect number satisfying the state, or splits into two exhaustive cases; each closing rule derives a contradiction from facts recorded in the state. The conclusion covers only odd perfect numbers that satisfy the root facts of the state.

OddPerfect.Cert.exclude_four_exact_squares

theorem exclude_four_exact_squares {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N)
  (h3 : N.factorization 3 = 2) (h7 : N.factorization 7 = 2)
  (h13 : N.factorization 13 = 2) (h19 : N.factorization 19 = 2) : False

Replay of OPNStarter.exclude_four_exact_squares through the checker: the components 7 ^ 2, 13 ^ 2, 19 ^ 2 each contribute a factor 3, overflowing the exponent 2 at 3.

OddPerfect.Cert.exclude_three_sq_five_sq

theorem exclude_three_sq_five_sq {N : ℕ} (h : OddPerfect N)
  (h3 : N.factorization 3 = 2) (h5 : N.factorization 5 = 2) : False

No odd perfect number has exponent exactly 2 at both 3 and 5. The certificate forces 13 ∣ N and 31 ∣ N, then splits the exponent of 13: exponent 1 forces 7 ∣ N and 3 ^ 2 * 5 ^ 2 * 13 * 7 is abundant; otherwise 3 ^ 2 * 5 ^ 2 * 13 ^ 2 * 31 is abundant.

OddPerfect.Pratt

OddPerfect/Pratt.lean · 184 lines · 12 public theorems tool

Pratt primality certificates checked by kernel reduction.

Proof idea. An entry (p, a, fs) claims that fs lists the prime factors of p − 1 with multiplicity and that a has order p − 1 modulo p. The checker verifies the product, that ap−1 ≡ 1 and a(p−1)/f ≢ 1 (mod p) for each f, by fast modular exponentiation, and that every f is an earlier entry or passes trial division; mathlib's lucas_primality turns an accepted entry into primality.

  powModAux n fuel b e acc = acc * b ^ e % n

Correctness of powModAux under sufficient fuel.

OddPerfect.Spoof

OddPerfect/Spoof.lean · 339 lines · 30 public theorems · imports Euler tool

The Descartes spoof and small local examples, kept as a regression suite for proposed arguments.

Proof idea. IsSpoofOddPerfect is a conjunction of five predicates on a list of pairs: every base odd and at least 3 with positive exponent; bases pairwise coprime; formal divisor sum equal to twice the value; and exactly one odd exponent, at a base ≡ 1 (mod 4) with exponent ≡ 1 (mod 4). For the Descartes list 3²·7²·11²·13²·22021 the file proves this conjunction by decide, plus Touchard's congruence for the value, value ≡ 1 (mod 4), at least three bases, and formal valuation conservation at every prime. Primality of the bases is not among the predicates: 22021 = 19²·61, so the value 198585576189 has six distinct prime factors, and the file proves it is not perfect (σ = 426027470778, while twice the value is 397171152378).

Public theorems

OddPerfect.Spoof.geom_eq_sum

theorem geom_eq_sum (b e : ℕ) : geom b e = ∑ i ∈ Finset.range (e + 1), b ^ i

geom agrees with the geometric sum used by mathlib.

OddPerfect.Spoof.sigma_prime_pow

theorem sigma_prime_pow {p : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (e : ℕ) : σ 1 (p ^ e) = geom p e

For a prime p, the divisor sum of p ^ e is geom p e.

OddPerfect.Spoof.isSpoofOddPerfect_descartes

theorem isSpoofOddPerfect_descartes : IsSpoofOddPerfect descartes

The Descartes list satisfies the five predicates of IsSpoofOddPerfect (odd bases at least 3 with positive exponents, pairwise coprime bases, formal divisor sum twice the value, Euler's parity pattern), kernel checked by decide. Primality of the bases is not among them.

OddPerfect.Spoof.formalSigma_eq_sigma

theorem formalSigma_eq_sigma (l : List (ℕ × ℕ)) (hp : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1.Prime)
  (hd : (l.map Prod.fst).Nodup) : formalSigma l = σ 1 (value l)

If every base is prime and the bases are pairwise distinct, the formal divisor sum is the true divisor sum of the value.

  (hp : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1.Prime) : OddPerfect (value l)

A spoof all of whose bases are prime is a genuine odd perfect number.

OddPerfect.Spoof.valuation_balance

theorem valuation_balance {l : List (ℕ × ℕ)} (h : IsSpoofOddPerfect l) (q : ℕ) :
  Nat.factorization 2 q + (value l).factorization q =
    (l.map fun x => (geom x.1 x.2).factorization q).sum

Formal valuation conservation: for every spoof and every q, the analog of OPNStarter.valuation_balance holds with component sums geom b e. It holds for every q, in particular at every base that is actually prime, so this bookkeeping alone cannot rule out a spoof.

σ(3^4) = 11^2: a component divisor sum can be a perfect power.

OddPerfect.Spoof.coprime_sigma_three_pow_four_thirteen

theorem coprime_sigma_three_pow_four_thirteen : Nat.Coprime (σ 1 (3 ^ 4)) 13

σ(3^4) = 121 is coprime to 13: 3^2 ∣ N does not force 13 ∣ N; the exact exponent matters.

OddPerfect.Spoof.eighteen_sq_add

theorem eighteen_sq_add : 18 ^ 2 + 18 + 1 = 7 ^ 3 ∧ ¬ Nat.Prime 18

18^2 + 18 + 1 = 7^3, so x^2 + x + 1 can be a perfect cube (here 18 is not prime).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt

OddPerfect/SpoofInt.lean · 765 lines · 72 public theorems · imports Spoof, Abundancy, Valuation tool

Integer spoof factorizations with negative bases allowed, and the signed spoof 3⁴·7²·11²·19²·(−127) tested against each predicate separately, with residues read either on the signed bases or on their absolute values (for example −127 ≡ 1 but 127 ≡ 3 modulo 4). Positivity lemmas separate genuine numbers from signed spoofs.

Proof idea. The structural conditions are separate predicates on a list of integer pairs, so the signed spoof is tested against each one by computation. Formal conservation holds for every spoof because the formal divisor sum is a product, so q-adic valuations of absolute values add. Positivity separates genuine numbers: a genuine component has σ(pe)/pe between 1 and p/(p − 1), while the formal factor of (−127)¹ is (1 − 127)/(−127) = 126/127 < 1. Hence the positive part 3⁴·7²·11²·19² = 173369889 of the signed spoof has abundancy 127/63 > 2, an abundant divisor that a genuine odd perfect number cannot have.

Public theorems

For odd bases, 2 ^ c divides the formal divisor sum, where c is the number of list positions carrying an odd exponent.

With odd bases and at least two list positions of odd exponent (equal entries counted separately), the formal divisor sum is divisible by 4.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.countP_odd_exponent_le_one

A spoof with odd bases has at most one list position of odd exponent: otherwise 4 ∣ formalSigma l = 2 * value l with value l odd.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.steuerwald_hyp_fails_voight

theorem steuerwald_hyp_fails_voight : ¬ ∀ x ∈ voight, x.2 % 2 = 0 → x.2 = 2

Steuerwald does not apply: not every even exponent equals 2 (base 3 has exponent 4). Equivalently the analog of exists_even_exponent_ge_four holds.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.exists_even_exponent_ge_four_voight

theorem exists_even_exponent_ge_four_voight : ∃ x ∈ voight, x.2 % 2 = 0 ∧ 4 ≤ x.2

theorem mcdaniel_hyp_fails_voight : ¬ ∀ x ∈ voight, x.2 % 2 = 0 → x.2 % 3 = 2

McDaniel does not apply: 4 % 3 = 1, so not every even exponent is ≡ 2 (mod 3).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.components_voight

theorem components_voight :
  geom 3 4 = 11 ^ 2 ∧ geom 7 2 = 3 * 19 ∧ geom 11 2 = 7 * 19 ∧ geom 19 2 = 3 * 127 ∧
    geom (-127) 1 = -2 * 3 ^ 2 * 7

The formal components: σ~(3^4) = 11^2, σ~(7^2) = 3 * 19, σ~(11^2) = 7 * 19, σ~(19^2) = 3 * 127, σ~((-127)^1) = -2 * 3^2 * 7.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.two_adic_voight

theorem two_adic_voight :
  (∀ x ∈ voight, x.2 % 2 = 0 → geom x.1 x.2 % 2 = 1) ∧ geom (-127) 1 % 4 = 2

The 2-adic part of Euler's argument: every component sum is odd except the one at the odd exponent, which is ≡ 2 (mod 4).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.hamiltonian_cycle_voight

theorem hamiltonian_cycle_voight :
  (11 : ℤ) ∣ geom 3 4 ∧ (7 : ℤ) ∣ geom 11 2 ∧ (19 : ℤ) ∣ geom 7 2 ∧ (-127 : ℤ) ∣ geom 19 2 ∧
    (3 : ℤ) ∣ geom (-127) 1

Strong connectivity of the formal graph (edge p → r when r divides the component sum at p): the Hamiltonian cycle 3 → 11 → 7 → 19 → -127 → 3 passes through every base.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.three_adic_ledger_voight

theorem three_adic_ledger_voight :
  (3 ^ 4 : ℤ) ∣ value voight ∧ ¬ (3 ^ 5 : ℤ) ∣ value voight ∧
    ¬ (3 : ℤ) ∣ geom 3 4 ∧ (3 : ℤ) ∣ geom 7 2 ∧ ¬ (3 ^ 2 : ℤ) ∣ geom 7 2 ∧
    ¬ (3 : ℤ) ∣ geom 11 2 ∧ (3 : ℤ) ∣ geom 19 2 ∧ ¬ (3 ^ 2 : ℤ) ∣ geom 19 2 ∧
    (3 ^ 2 : ℤ) ∣ geom (-127) 1 ∧ ¬ (3 ^ 3 : ℤ) ∣ geom (-127) 1

The 3-adic exponent of the value is exactly 4, and the component sums carry exactly 3^0, 3^1, 3^0, 3^1, 3^2, so the ledger balances: 4 = 0 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 2.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.not_threeWeight_ledger_abs_voight

theorem not_threeWeight_ledger_abs_voight :
  (voight.map fun x => threeWeight x.1.natAbs x.2).sum = 2 ∧
    ¬ (3 ^ 2 ∣ (value voight).natAbs ∧ ¬ 3 ^ 3 ∣ (value voight).natAbs)

The closed form 3-adic ledger read on absolute values fails: threeWeight applied to |b| sums to 2 (since 127 ≡ 1 (mod 3) with exponent 1 contributes v_3(2) = 0), but v_3(|value|) = 4.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.threeWeight_ledger_signed_voight

theorem threeWeight_ledger_signed_voight :
  ((-127 : ℤ) % 3 = 2 ∧ padicValNat 3 ((-127 + 1 : ℤ).natAbs) + padicValNat 3 2 = 2) ∧
    threeWeight 3 4 + threeWeight 7 2 + threeWeight 11 2 + threeWeight 19 2 + 2 = 4

The closed form 3-adic ledger with signed residues balances: -127 ≡ 2 (mod 3) at odd exponent 1 contributes v_3(|-127 + 1|) + v_3(2) = 2, and the other bases contribute 0 + 1 + 0 + 1, total 4 = v_3(|value|).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.natAbs_list_prod

theorem natAbs_list_prod (L : List ℤ) : L.prod.natAbs = (L.map Int.natAbs).prod

natAbs of a list product is the product of the natAbs.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.valuation_balance

theorem valuation_balance {l : List (ℤ × ℕ)} (h : IsSpoofPerfect l) (hb : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1 ≠ 0)
  (q : ℕ) :
  Nat.factorization 2 q + (value l).natAbs.factorization q =
    (l.map fun x => (geom x.1 x.2).natAbs.factorization q).sum

Formal valuation conservation for integer spoofs: for every q, the analog of OPNStarter.valuation_balance holds with the absolute values of the component sums. It holds at every q, in particular at every base that is a signed prime, so this bookkeeping (and every identity derived from formalSigma = 2 * value and multiplicativity over the bases) cannot distinguish a genuine odd perfect number from a signed spoof. The only hypothesis besides the spoof equation is that no base is zero.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.valuation_balance_voight

theorem valuation_balance_voight (q : ℕ) :
  Nat.factorization 2 q + (value voight).natAbs.factorization q =
    (voight.map fun x => (geom x.1 x.2).natAbs.factorization q).sum

The signed spoof satisfies formal valuation conservation at every q.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isSpoofPerfect_append_iff

theorem isSpoofPerfect_append_iff (l : List (ℤ × ℕ)) (c : ℤ) :
  IsSpoofPerfect (l ++ [(-(2 * c + 1), 1)]) ↔ c * formalSigma l = (2 * c + 1) * value l

Appending the base -(2c+1) at exponent 1: the result is spoof perfect iff c * formalSigma l = (2c+1) * value l. No hypothesis on l or c.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_iff_isSpoofPerfect

theorem sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_iff_isSpoofPerfect {l : List (ℕ × ℕ)}
  (hp : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1.Prime) (hd : (l.map Prod.fst).Nodup) {k : ℕ} (hk : 0 < k) :
  (k ∣ Spoof.value l ∧ σ 1 (Spoof.value l) = 2 * Spoof.value l + Spoof.value l / k) ↔
    IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat l ++ [(-(2 * (k : ℤ) + 1), 1)])

Arithmetic identity behind the near perfect link. For a factorization l with distinct prime bases and value m, and k > 0: k ∣ m and σ(m) = 2m + m/k iff m * (-(2k+1))^1 is a spoof. This is not a classification of near perfect numbers: at k = 1 the amount m / k = m is not a proper divisor (see isNearPerfectWith_iff_isSpoofPerfect for 1 < k). Neither coprimality of 2k+1 with m nor oddness of m is needed (coprimality is only a side condition for the extended spoof); k ∣ m is automatic on the spoof side since gcd(k, 2k+1) = 1.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.div_lt_self_iff_one_lt

theorem div_lt_self_iff_one_lt {m k : ℕ} (hm : 0 < m) (hk : 0 < k) : m / k < m ↔ 1 < k

For 0 < m and 0 < k, the amount m / k is less than m exactly when 1 < k.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isNearPerfectWith_iff_isSpoofPerfect

theorem isNearPerfectWith_iff_isSpoofPerfect {l : List (ℕ × ℕ)} (hp : ∀ x ∈ l, x.1.Prime)
  (hd : (l.map Prod.fst).Nodup) {k : ℕ} (hk : 1 < k) :
  (k ∣ Spoof.value l ∧ IsNearPerfectWith (Spoof.value l) (Spoof.value l / k)) ↔
    IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat l ++ [(-(2 * (k : ℤ) + 1), 1)])

The near perfect link. For a factorization l with distinct prime bases and value m, and 1 < k: k ∣ m and m is near perfect with omitted proper divisor m / k iff m * (-(2k+1))^1 is a spoof.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.prime_johnson

theorem prime_johnson : ∀ x ∈ johnson, x.1.Prime

johnson has prime bases.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.nearPerfect_johnson

theorem nearPerfect_johnson :
  Spoof.value johnson = 173369889 ∧ 63 ∣ Spoof.value johnson ∧
    σ 1 (Spoof.value johnson) = 2 * Spoof.value johnson + Spoof.value johnson / 63

σ(173369889) = 2 * 173369889 + 173369889 / 63, with 63 ∣ 173369889.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isNearPerfectWith_johnson

theorem isNearPerfectWith_johnson :
  63 ∣ Spoof.value johnson ∧ IsNearPerfectWith (Spoof.value johnson) (Spoof.value johnson / 63) ∧
    Spoof.value johnson / 63 = 2751903

The omitted divisor 173369889 / 63 = 2751903 is a proper divisor.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.voight_eq_johnson

theorem voight_eq_johnson : voight = ofNat johnson ++ [(-(2 * (63 : ℕ) + 1 : ℤ), 1)]

The signed spoof is the near perfect number 173369889 with the extra base -(2 * 63 + 1).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isSpoofPerfect_voight_of_johnson

theorem isSpoofPerfect_voight_of_johnson : IsSpoofPerfect voight

The signed spoof recovered through the near perfect link.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_iff_isSpoofPerfect_nat

theorem sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_iff_isSpoofPerfect_nat {m k : ℕ} (hm : m ≠ 0) (hk : 0 < k) :
  (k ∣ m ∧ σ 1 m = 2 * m + m / k) ↔
    IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat (m.primeFactors.toList.map fun p => (p, m.factorization p)) ++
      [(-(2 * (k : ℤ) + 1), 1)])

The arithmetic identity sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_iff_isSpoofPerfect stated for a natural number m ≠ 0 through its prime factorization list, for 0 < k. At k = 1 the amount m / k = m is not a proper divisor.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isNearPerfectWith_iff_isSpoofPerfect_nat

theorem isNearPerfectWith_iff_isSpoofPerfect_nat {m k : ℕ} (hm : m ≠ 0) (hk : 1 < k) :
  (k ∣ m ∧ IsNearPerfectWith m (m / k)) ↔
    IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat (m.primeFactors.toList.map fun p => (p, m.factorization p)) ++
      [(-(2 * (k : ℤ) + 1), 1)])

The near perfect link for a natural number m ≠ 0 and 1 < k: k ∣ m and m is near perfect with omitted proper divisor m / k iff m * (-(2k+1))^1 is a spoof, with m written through its prime factorization list.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isNearPerfect_iff_exists_isSpoofPerfect

theorem isNearPerfect_iff_exists_isSpoofPerfect {m : ℕ} (hm : m ≠ 0) :
  IsNearPerfect m ↔ ∃ k : ℕ, 1 < k ∧
    IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat (m.primeFactors.toList.map fun p => (p, m.factorization p)) ++
      [(-(2 * (k : ℤ) + 1), 1)])

Every near perfect number m ≠ 0 arises from the link: with omitted divisor d, the cofactor k = m / d satisfies 1 < k, and the extension by -(2k+1) is a spoof.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.sigma_120

theorem sigma_120 : σ 1 120 = 360

σ(120) = 360.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_120

theorem sigma_eq_two_mul_add_div_120 : 1 ∣ 120 ∧ σ 1 120 = 2 * 120 + 120 / 1

The arithmetic identity holds at m = 120, k = 1: σ(120) = 2 * 120 + 120 / 1.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.not_isNearPerfectWith_120_120

theorem not_isNearPerfectWith_120_120 : ¬ IsNearPerfectWith 120 120

The omitted amount 120 at k = 1 is not a proper divisor of 120.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.not_isNearPerfect_120

theorem not_isNearPerfect_120 : ¬ IsNearPerfect 120

120 is not near perfect: σ(120) = 2 * 120 + d forces d = 120, which is not proper.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.abundancy_prime_pow_bounds

theorem abundancy_prime_pow_bounds {p e : ℕ} (hp : p.Prime) (he : 0 < e) :
  1 < abundancy (p ^ e) ∧ abundancy (p ^ e) < (p : ℚ) / (p - 1)

Genuine side: for a prime p and e > 0, 1 < σ(p^e)/p^e < p/(p-1).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.formalFactor_neg_lt_one

theorem formalFactor_neg_lt_one {b : ℤ} (hb : b ≤ -2) :
  0 < (geom b 1 : ℚ) / b ∧ (geom b 1 : ℚ) / b < 1

Spoof side, general: for a base b ≤ -2 at exponent 1, the formal factor (1 + b)/b lies strictly between 0 and 1.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.formalFactor_voight_lt_one

theorem formalFactor_voight_lt_one :
  (geom (-127) 1 : ℚ) / (-127) = 126 / 127 ∧ (geom (-127) 1 : ℚ) / (-127) < 1

The lower bound 1 < σ(p^e)/p^e of genuine components fails for a negative base.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.formalFactor_neg_three_sq

theorem formalFactor_neg_three_sq :
  (geom (-3) 2 : ℚ) / (-3) ^ 2 = 7 / 9 ∧
    (-3 : ℚ) / (-3 - 1) < (geom (-3) 2 : ℚ) / (-3) ^ 2

A negative base at even exponent breaks the upper bound: the formal factor of (-3)^2 is 7/9 > 3/4 = (-3)/((-3) - 1).

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.pow_lt_geom

theorem pow_lt_geom {b : ℤ} (hb : 0 < b) {e : ℕ} (he : 0 < e) : b ^ e < geom b e

For positive bases and positive exponents the component sum exceeds the power, so the formal abundancy of a positive factorization strictly increases when a factor is appended. This monotonicity is the input of every abundant divisor argument.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.abundant_divisor_voight

theorem abundant_divisor_voight :
  abundancy 173369889 = 127 / 63 ∧ 2 < abundancy 173369889 ∧
    (173369889 : ℤ) ∣ value voight

The negative factor 126/127 brings the formal abundancy back to 2.

The list 3^2 7^2 7^2 13^1 (-19)^2 is spoof perfect.

The list 3^2 7^2 7^2 13^1 (-19)^2 has odd bases of absolute value above one and positive exponents.

OddPerfect.SpoofInt.isSpoofPerfect_descartes

  IsSpoofPerfect (ofNat Spoof.descartes) ∧ BasesPos (ofNat Spoof.descartes)

The Descartes spoof lifted to ℤ is spoof perfect, with positive bases.
